#199. Judge orders Iowa to permit Satanic Temple holiday event at state capitol

Federal court says group likely to win its free speech claim and tells officials to approve application within seven days

A federal judge ordered state officials in Iowa on Monday to permit a local Satanic Temple organization to hold a holiday event in the rotunda of the state capitol after years of denying the group’s application.

The religious organization and Iowa Republican state officials have been in a legal battle over the group’s applications to hold a holiday event at the state capitol in recent years.

Republicans in Iowa, who currently control the governorship and majorities in the state house and senate, have cited the capitol building’s policies prohibiting gratuitous violence and obscene displays as justification for denying the applications.

According to the office of the attorney general, Brenna Bird, state Republicans intend to appeal the federal judge’s decision.

The Satanic Temple and Iowa chapter leader filed a lawsuit in August, alleging discrimination from former and current state officials.

Chief District Judge Stephanie M Rose stated in her court order that the group was likely to succeed on its free speech claim and ordered the current director of the Iowa department of administrative services to approve the holiday event application within seven days.

The Satanic Temple (TST), which was founded in Salem, Massachusetts, in 2013, characterizes itself as a “non-theistic religious organization” that advocates for secularism and emphasizes separation from the Church of Satan founded in 1966.

In December 2023, the group had a religious holiday display in the Iowa state capitol, but it was vandalized by a Christian military veteran. The Turning Point founder, Charlie Kirk, pledged $10,000 to the accused vandal’s legal defense. The following year, the Satanic Temple’s application for a holiday display and event was denied.

“State officials have made it clear that they denied TST permission to hold events in the Capitol rotunda because they disagreed with their religious viewpoint,” said Shefali Aurora, ACLU of Iowa staff attorney, in a statement on the court order. “It is a foundational principle that the government cannot restrict speech in this way, favor one religion over another, or discriminate against religious groups because they don’t agree with them.”

The group said its proposed holiday event would include costumes of the European folklore character Krampus, caroling, coloring for children and ornament making. In its denial of the application, state officials cited sticks included in the costumes that could be used as weapons and policies meant to protect children who might be present in the public space.

Judge Rose wrote that the state officials’ arguments did not justify the denials, noting the argument that the character Krampus might scare children was “not a permissible basis for excluding speech” and the argument about the sticks included with the costumes “cannot justify denying the entire event”.

The Iowa department of administrative services and the Republican governor, Kim Reynolds, have not yet commented on the court order.

In 2023, Governor Reynolds stated in response to the group’s holiday display at the state capitol, “like many Iowans, I find the Satanic Temple’s display in the Capitol absolutely objectionable. In a free society, the best response to objectionable speech is more speech, and I encourage all those of faith to join me today in praying over the Capitol and recognizing the nativity scene that will be on display – the true reason for the season.”

#195. US midterm elections 2026 live: Americans go to the polls in referendum on Trump’s agenda

Polls open across the US where key Senate and House races will decide which party controls Congress for remainder of Donald Trump’s second term

What are the races to watch and why do they matter?

The US Congress has two chambers: the House of Representatives and the Senate. Both must approve federal legislation before it can become law and both play important roles in overseeing the executive branch.

The House is elected every two years, meaning all 435 voting seats are contested at every congressional election. The Senate has 100 members serving staggered six-year terms, so roughly a third of its seats are up for election every two years.

All 435 seats in the House of Representatives. Republicans are defending a narrow majority, with Democrats needing a net gain of four seats to take control.

35 seats in the Senate. Thirty-three seats are up in regular elections, along with two special elections in Florida and Ohio to fill the remainder of terms that were vacated early. Republicans currently hold 53 seats, against 47 held by Democrats and independents who caucus with them. Democrats need to gain four seats to secure a majority.

39 governorships. Voters will choose governors in 36 states and three US territories.

Thousands of state legislative seats, as well as local offices, judicial contests and ballot measures on issues including taxes, abortion rights and voting rules.

The elections for Congress will determine which party controls Congress for the final two years of Trump’s second term. The governorships and state legislative contests will shape policymaking closer to home, from education and reproductive rights to taxation and the administration of elections.

The midterms have a long history of punishing the occupant of the White House. The president’s party has lost House seats in 18 of the past 20 midterm elections, a 90% rate of loss that stretches back to 1946.

An issue with ballot scanners in Westchester County, New York – part of the state’s competitive 17th Congressional district – has snarled voting on Tuesday morning.

“My office is monitoring this issue with scanners in Westchester and is in touch with the state and local Boards of Elections as they work on a solution,” New York Attorney General Letitia James said in a statement posted to social media. “Westchester voters can still safely fill out and turn in their ballots at their polling locations. Your votes will count.”

The race for NY-17 pits Mike Lawler, a moderate Republican incumbent, against Democratic nominee Cait Conley. It’s one of the most closely-watched races in the country, and a potential measure of whether moderate Republicans, like Lawler, who have broken with Trump on occasion can overcome an unpopular president.

Both Lawler and Conley posted statements to social media urging voters to stay in line, assuring them the issue would be sorted out and every vote would be counted.

“The Polls are open and operational, and every vote cast will be counted,” Conley wrote. “Please stay in line and have your voice heard as they work to resolve the issue.”

Thirty three people lined up at Roswell Library at 7am, waiting for the polls to open in a well-contested district in the northern suburbs of Atlanta, Georgia. Jim Shannon, a small business owner, found himself at the wrong precinct.

“The idea that I’ve voted here every time … maybe they think they can discourage people. I don’t know,” he said. Shannon is a political conservative who voted for Rep. Mike Collins for the US Senate and Rick Jackson for governor.

“I believe the government needs to hire forensic accountants and go through every agency and find the waste, because taxpayers are paying for all of this.”

On 5 November, Guardian columnist Jonathan Freedland will join our global editor Devika Bhat and the Edward Orsborn Professor of US Politics & Political History, Prof Adam Smith, in London, along with Mary Trump, clinical psychologist, author, and political commentator, Washington bureau chief David Smith and leading US commentator Tara Setmayer live from the US.

They will discuss the election results and their implications for Trump’s future, the US and global politics. You can book tickets – in person or livestream.

Here are some of the latest images sent to us over the newswires as Americans cast their votes in the midterm elections, an effective referendum on Donald Trump’s presidency that will determine control of Congress:

As we have been reporting, the first results are expected after 18.00 EST (15.00 PST or 23.00 GMT), although these won’t tell us the national picture. You can keep an eye on our live tracker to keep up with the latest results when they come through:

The supreme court on Monday ruled against the Trump administration and its attempts to force California election officials to segregate and not count ballots from people that the justice department claims are non-citizens.

The ruling follows several lower courts siding with California officials, who argued the federal government was seeking to interfere in state elections.

The court did not issue an explanation for its ruling, which is customary in cases that come to it on an emergency basis. There were no noted dissents from the justices.

California and the Trump administration have been in a legal standoff over the past few weeks following the justice department’s claim that 20,000 non-citizens were on the state’s voter rolls.

The DoJ delivered a USB drive to Los Angeles and Sacramento election officials in mid-October with the names, giving only 48 hours for officials to review the list and threatening to sue if the ballots were not sequestered until those listed could verify their citizenship. Election officials refused, leading to a series of courtroom battles.

The justice department has shared a list of 126 jurisdictions across the country to which election monitors have been sent. In a press release this morning, the DoJ said the monitors will be deployed to all 50 states for the first time in what the department described as its “largest monitoring effort in history”.

As my colleague Sam Levine notes in this story, the DoJ has aggressively inserted itself into the midterm elections in the contest’s final weeks, shattering the department’s longstanding practice of political neutrality.

The midterms are occurring amid uncertainty about whether Trump, who still refuses to acknowledge his 2020 presidential election loss, will accept the results if they do not go the Republicans’ way, as many polls predict they won’t do.

He has urged Republicans to “nationalize” voting, pressed for restrictions on mail-in ballots and deployed federal law enforcement to pursue discredited fraud claims. At a Republican Party convention in September, the deeply unpopular president called on supporters to “cheat like hell” whether they’re “registered or not”.

For a special episode of Politics Weekly America, Guardian columnist Jonathan Freedland is joined by Alexandra Petri, a staff writer from the Atlantic, about this election cycle’s most effective ad campaigns, surprising alliances and embarrassing gaffes. You can listen to it in full here:

In Florida, 18 polling sites have been moved due to flooding, according to the secretary of state’s office. The affected counties are Brevard, Highlands, Indian River, Martin, Okeechobee, Palm Beach and St. Lucie. The alternative polling sites include community centers, a council chambers, a town hall and a church. The announcement was made before polls opened in Florida at 7am ET on Tuesday morning.

“For the safety and security of our election workers and the voters of Brevard, it is necessary to relocate a couple of precinct polling locations due to these unprecedented circumstances,” Tim Bobanic, Brevard County supervisor of elections, was quoted as having said in a statement.

The state’s governor Ron DeSantis declared a state of emergency for several counties on Monday after heavy rain turned roads into rivers and submerged cars.

In Florida, progressive Democrat Angie Nixon is hoping to pull off an upset and unseat Republican incumbent Ashley Moody in one of two special elections this cycle.

Another interesting race to watch is that of Republican lawmaker Maria Elvira Salazar, who faces a challenge from Democrat Eliott Rodriguez, a former television news anchor.

Salazar, whose district has a large Latino population, including Miami’s historic Little Havana neighbourhood, made headlines earlier this month when she warned Trump that his hardline immigration enforcement policies had gone too far.

It has just gone past 06:30 in North Carolina, Ohio and West Virginia where polls have now opened.

Here are some of the key voter issues shaping the US midterm elections:

The economy: Despite Trump’s 2024 campaign pledge to tackle affordability issues, the economy consistently ranks as voters’ top concern.

Iran: Trump’s decision to go to war with the Tehran regime has exacerbated economic worries. Iran’s retaliatory decision to close the strait of Hormuz has driven up gasoline prices for US motorists. It also represents a breach of his pledge to end the US’s supposed addiction to “forever wars”.

Immigration: Enforcement and closing the border remains a signature issue for Trump. He repeatedly boasts of the impact of his closure of the southern border as one of his biggest achievements, making comparisons with the performance of Joe Biden.

AI and datacenters: AI has emerged as a key campaign issue, with surveys showing a majority of voters holding negative views on it, even as Trump champions its development and attempts to rename it “super intelligence”.

You can read more about the issues guiding the elections here:

A final set of polls from the New York Times and Siena University, published on Sunday, put Democratic candidates ahead in five of seven key battleground contests – including Texas and Kansas, where voters have not sent a Democrat to the Senate since 1988 and 1932, respectively.

The final stretch of the campaign was not without drama for the Democrats, however. In Michigan – widely seen as a crucial pillar to either party’s ambitions of control in the Senate – Democratic congresswoman Haley Stevens, who lost her party’s senate primary to Abdul El-Sayed, endorsed his Republican rival, Mike Rogers, on Sunday.

Stevens was widely condemned by fellow Democrats over the move, which followed a feud with the El-Sayed campaign over an outside group’s campaign ad that blamed the high cost of living on US support for Israel. Chuck Schumer, the Senate minority leader, described her endorsement of Rogers as “a despicable betrayal”.

“Whatever they’re trying to distract you, it’s because they’re picking your pocket,” El-Sayed told hundreds of supporters in Royal Oak, Michigan on Monday.

The extraordinary rupture in Michigan took places days after the party faced damaging headlines in Alaska, where it was reported that the Democratic senate candidate’s campaign was behind an effort to recruit a challenger with almost the same name as her rival, the Republican senator Dan Sullivan. (The Democratic candidate, Mary Peltola, has denied any knowledge of such a scheme on the part of her campaign.)

Before the report of the Peltola campaign’s involvement, the Department of Justice (DoJ) filed a lawsuit to block the certification of Alaska’s Senate results in the event of a Republican defeat, citing potential “voter confusion” over the presence of two Dan Sullivans on the ballot. The lawsuit has since been dismissed by a judge.

Voting is taking place today (Tuesday 3 November), but the results may not be known tonight. Close contests, different state rules for counting early and postal ballots, and possible recounts or legal challenges could delay the final outcome.

Once the dust has settled, there will be no respite in this – the land of the endless campaign. Attention will soon turn to the 2028 presidential election campaign, when both parties will seek to turn the results of this November into a foundation for the next fight for the White House.

Here’s an hour-by-hour rundown of when polls close around the US, courtesy of our blogger and reporter Shrai Popat:

The first polls have opened in the pivotal US midterm elections in the eastern state of Vermont, where it has just gone past 5.15am.

The next polls to open will be at 6am ET (in 40 minutes) including in: Connecticut, Indiana, Kentucky, Maine, New Jersey, New York and Virginia.

The first results will start to come in after 18:00 EST (23:00 GMT) but a tight race could take hours to call – or even days.

It is important to remember that more than 58m early votes have already been cast across the US, according to an analysis by the University of Florida, a huge increase in the total number of early votes made in the last midterm elections, in 2022.

Rachel Leingang, a Midwest political correspondent for Guardian US, has been in Iowa, getting a sense of the political mood ahead of the crcuial elections:

Across Iowa on Monday, candidates made final pitches in a set of toss-up races up and down the ballot, noting just how close these contests were and how pivotal the state could be for the nation’s politics.

In a standing-room-only town hall in Waverly, Iowa, Democratic gubernatorial hopeful Rob Sand started off by asking people in the crowd which political party they belong to, then having people clap for all of them before directing the audience to sing “America the Beautiful” together.

The bipartisan tone underscores Sand’s pitch to Iowa voters, including those who would need to cross party lines to support him: “not redder or bluer, but better and truer”.

Wearing shirts that urged people to vote for Democrats and “rotate the crops”, about 150 voters turned out to the rally in the Republican-leaning area. Sand told the Guardian that throughout the hundreds of town halls he has seen a “yearning” from people for their politicians to work together to solve problems, even as voters are generally angry this year.

“We need to focus that anger and point it at where it ought to be pointed, which is at a broken two-choice political system that is too easily abused to allow elected officials to be corrupt and to be lazy and still hold power,” he said. “A lot of politicians want people to be angry with their neighbors.”

Down in the Des Moines suburbs in the early evening, as rain poured down and the sun set before 6pm, Sarah Booz knocked doors for Democratic candidates. On the doors, there were still a lot of undecided voters.

Some told her they’d be voting for Sand and the rest of the downballot Democrats, while others said they needed to do more research. One man who said he normally votes red said the gubernatorial race would probably be a last-minute decision.

And in the evening, at a Holiday Inn by the Des Moines airport, much of the Republican ticket rallied, culminating in a pitch from US Senate candidate Ashley Hinson. Chuck Grassley, the Republican US senator and the oldest sitting US senator, took digs at Sand, saying that the state government is well run and “we don’t need Sand in that machinery”. He also called Democrats running across the country “outlandish socialists” who want to “transform America”.

“I hope that you’ll do everything you can to make sure that I have a colleague from Iowa that won’t neutralize my vote,” he said, advocating for Hinson. Hinson, taking the stage in front of a bus she’s been campaigning in, called her opponent, Zach Turek, “Too Liberal Turek”. “We fire liberal phonies, we don’t promote them,” she said.

The US reportedly attacked Qeshm Island in southern Iran on Monday after Donald Trump downplayed the chances of a return to war with Tehran after the midterm elections.

“We had a war with Iran. We won,” Trump told journalists yesterday. “I don’t know if you even call it a war because we haven’t been doing any fighting for months.”

Monday’s strikes, first reported by the Wall Street Journal, were defensive in nature, according to officials, and targeted surface-to-air missiles being used against US assets, Reuters and the Associated Press reported.

But undermining Trump’s comments to the press, the WSJ reported that the US has been launching smaller-scale strikes against Iran for several weeks, but have not been publicly announcing the attacks as they did at the start of the conflict in February.

On Monday, US vice-president JD Vance claimed there had been “a number of good conversations with the Iranians” and that he was feeling good about the prospects of a “negotiated settlement”. But despite this talk of diplomatic progress, Iran has continued to enforce its closure of the strait of Hormuz and in recent weeks Trump has hinted at renewing attacks on Iran ​after ⁠the midterm elections are over.

Voters now have the chance to express their frustration over the inflationary impact of the president’s ill-judged war, which has sent fuel prices soaring and shown the limits of American power.

The US Congress has two chambers: the House of Representatives and the Senate. Both must approve federal legislation before it can become law and both play important roles in overseeing the executive branch.

The House is elected every two years, meaning all 435 voting seats are contested at every congressional election. The Senate has 100 members serving staggered six-year terms, so roughly a third of its seats are up for election every two years.

All 435 seats in the House of Representatives. Republicans are defending a narrow majority, with Democrats needing a net gain of four seats to take control.

35 seats in the Senate. Thirty-three seats are up in regular elections, along with two special elections in Florida and Ohio to fill the remainder of terms that were vacated early. Republicans currently hold 53 seats, against 47 held by Democrats and independents who caucus with them. Democrats need to gain four seats to secure a majority.

39 governorships. Voters will choose governors in 36 states and three US territories.

Thousands of state legislative seats, as well as local offices, judicial contests and ballot measures on issues including taxes, abortion rights and voting rules.

The elections for Congress will determine which party controls Congress for the final two years of Trump’s second term. The governorships and state legislative contests will shape policymaking closer to home, from education and reproductive rights to taxation and the administration of elections.

The midterms have a long history of punishing the occupant of the White House. The president’s party has lost House seats in 18 of the past 20 midterm elections, a 90% rate of loss that stretches back to 1946.

Donald Trump will still be US president on the morning of 4 November. But he could be facing a very different political landscape, depending on the results of the midterm elections held the previous day.

As the name implies, midterm elections fall nearly halfway through a president’s term. They are often a telling referendum on the sitting president’s performance, something Trump is actively encouraging by telling voters “pretend I’m on the ballot”. In this case, voters will decide whether Trump’s Republican party can retain its grip on Congress.

Democrats are hoping to capitalise on growing discontent with Trump to seize control of the US House of Representatives, and possibly the Senate. Republicans are fighting to preserve their congressional majorities and prevent a fresh period of legislative paralysis and investigations into the White House.

Several Senate contests have emerged as key battlegrounds. In Michigan, progressive Abdul El-Sayed is seeking to hold an open seat for Democrats against Republican Mike Rogers, who received a last-minute endorsement from Democrat Haley Stevens. In Georgia, Jon Ossoff, a Democratic senator, enjoys a healthy poll lead over Republican challenger Mike Collins.

In Maine, a state won by Democrat Kamala Harris in 2024, Susan Collins, the Republican senator, is defending her seat against Democrat Troy Jackson. Ohio offers another major test, with Democrat Sherrod Brown seeking to return to the Senate against Republican Jon Husted.

Texas has become especially closely watched. Democrat James Talarico is taking on Republican Ken Paxton, the state’s attorney general. Republicans have spent about $210m to help the scandal-plagued Paxton. No Democrat has won a statewide election in Texas since 1994. Trump won Texas by nearly 14 points in 2024.

In Alaska, Democrat Mary Peltola’s Senate race against Republican incumbent Dan S Sullivan sparked controversy after Republicans accused the candidate of planting a second Dan Sullivan to confuse voters. A judge dismissed a Trump-backed lawsuit seeking to block certification of Alaska’s Senate race results if Dan Sullivan loses.

Other contests in Kansas, Iowa, North Carolina and New Hampshire could help determine which party controls the chamber (if there is a 50-50 split, JD Vance, the vice-president, a Republican, wields the tiebreaker vote). Latest polls show Democrats ahead in Maine, North Carolina and Kansas, and competitive in Ohio. Also on the map this year are South Carolina and Mississippi, two reliably Republican states that have seen aggressive Democratic challenges this year.

It’s Tuesday 3 November, which means that after months of campaigning, debate and analysis, the critical US midterm elections that will take the political temperature of the nation halfway through Donald Trump’s second term – and determine the trajectory of the remainder of his presidency – are finally upon us. Americans, who have already voted early in record numbers, will decide the balance of power in Congress and, therefore, Trump’s ability to advance his agenda for the next two years.

With the US president almost unprecedentedly unpopular, Democrats are aiming for what would be stunning victories in seats where, for decades, turning blue would have been unthinkable. Trump is not on the ballot, but this election is very much a referendum on the direction of the country under his Republican party.

Trump’s agenda has thrown the US into a deeply unpopular – and expensive – war alongside Israel against Iran, sparking a global energy crisis and regional conflict that remains unresolved. The war has exacerbated inflation and the cost-of-living crisis, which had already been worsened by Trump’s chaotic tariff policies. The un-affordability of basic living costs from groceries and gas to childcare, housing and healthcare has been a defining issue of this election, with concerns over the economy turning even deeply conservative seats competitive.

The second Trump administration has also overseen violent surges of federal immigration enforcement across Democrat-led cities, deeply divisive operations that killed two Americans earlier this year. The administration has pursued an aggressive and cruel mass deportation policy, slashed federal government agencies and Medicaid funding, supported the restriction of abortion rights in many states, and expanded presidential power in unprecedented ways.

And now, after months of gerrymandering, efforts to curtail voting rights, and scaremongering over election integrity as Republicans cling to power, also looming over this election is the question of whether Trump and his allies will accept the results.

This will be a defining moment for American democracy, so stick with us as we bring you all the latest news, analyses and commentary as it all unfolds.

#192. Micron Technology: China probes US chip maker for cybersecurity risks as tech tension escalates

China has launched a cybersecurity probe into Micron Technology, one of America’s largest memory chip makers, in apparent retaliation after US allies in Asia and Europe announced new restrictions on the sale of key technology to Beijing.

The Cyberspace Administration of China (CAC) will review products sold by Micron in the country, according to a statement by the watchdog late on Friday.

The move is aimed at “ensuring the security of key information infrastructure supply chains, preventing cybersecurity risks caused by hidden product problems, and maintaining national security,” it noted.

It came on the same day that Japan, a US ally, said it would restrict the export of advanced chip manufacturing equipment to countries including China, following similar moves by the United States and the Netherlands.

Washington and its allies have announced curbs on China’s semiconductor industry, which strike at the heart of Beijing’s bid to become a tech superpower.

Last month, the Netherlands also unveiled new restrictions on overseas sales of semiconductor technology, citing the need to protect national security. In October, the United States banned Chinese companies from buying advanced chips and chipmaking equipment without a license.

Micron told CNN it was aware of the review.

“We are in communication with the CAC and are cooperating fully,” it said, adding that it stands by the security of its products. “Micron’s product shipments, engineering, manufacturing, sales and other functions are operating as normal.”

Shares in Micron sank 4.4% on Wall Street Friday following the news, the biggest drop in more than three months. On Monday, they closed another 1.2% lower. Micron derives more than 10% of its revenue from China.

In an earlier filing, the Idaho-based company had warned of such risks.

“The Chinese government may restrict us from participating in the China market or may prevent us from competing effectively with Chinese companies,” it said last week.

Japan joins the US and Europe in chipmaking curbs on China

China has strongly criticized restrictions on tech exports, saying last month it “firmly opposes” such measures.

In efforts to boost growth and job creation, Beijing is seeking to woo foreign investments as it grapples with mounting economic challenges. The newly minted premier Li Qiang and several top economic officials have been rolling out the welcome wagon for global CEOs and promising they would “provide a good environment and services.”

But Beijing has also exerted growing pressure on foreign companies to bring them into line with its agenda.

Last month, authorities closed the Beijing office of Mintz Group, a US corporate intelligence firm, and detained five local staff.

Days earlier, they suspended Deloitte’s operations in Beijing for three months and imposed a fine of $31 million over alleged lapses in its work auditing a state-owned distressed debt manager.

CNN’s Michelle Toh, Junko Ogura, Sandi Sidhu and Chris Lau contributed to the article.

#190. US: Trump labels Democrats 'dangerous' ahead of midterms

US President Donald Trump said the Democrats nominated "the most extreme and dangerous" candidates in US history in the midterms. He also lowered expectations by saying a sitting president "doesn't win" the midterms.

US President Donald Trump labeled the upcoming midterm elections as the most important "in our lifetime."

Trump seemed to lower his supporters' expectations on Monday, saying that an incumbent president "doesn't win the midterms," while also adding that his Republican party "gave this a very strong shot."

"Usually, whoever the sitting president is, Republican or Democrat, good or bad, good president, great president. For some reason, the sitting president doesn't win the midterms," Trump said.

According to the US president, the Democrats nominated "the most extreme and dangerous" list of candidates in the country's history.

Senate Minority Leader Chuck Schumer, a Democrat, responded to Trump's accusation by calling his last message ahead of the midterms "pathetic."

"Donald Trump's pathetic closing message as his illegal war rages, his corruption mounts, and the inflation he caused persists," Schumer posted on his X account.

Trump's last-minute attempt to block 20,000 California ballots was rejected by the US Supreme Court.

Ahead of the midterms, the US carried out a strike at Iran's Qeshm Island, the Wall Street Journal reported, citing US sources.

The US Central Command did not officially confirm the attack.

This comes despite the Trump administration voicing optimism that the talks with Iran could lead to progress on ending the war between the two countries.

Vice President JD Vance earlier said that the goal remains to prevent Iran from securing nuclear weapons.

"We do think that there is a pathway there, but it really does require a firm commitment and a verifiable commitment that they don’t rebuild that nuclear program," Vance said, also labeling the US' conversations with Iran "good."

This comes as voters are set to decide control of Congress for the rest of Trump's term as president on November 3.

Millions of voters will head to the polls, with surging costs of living and energy prices as a result of the Trump-led war in Iran taking center stage.

Trump said he hoped that voters would realize the risks of Iran having nuclear weapons heading to the polls.

"If we allowed Iran to have a nuclear weapon, this country could never function the same way," he argued.

Oil prices dropped significantly as the US and Iran renewed the talks over ending the war between the two countries.

The midterms are largely seen as a referendum on the current US president's policies.

The Democrats are expected to retake the House of Representatives from the Republicans' control, but the fight for the Senate is considered to be close.

The election's outcome is bound to influence both parties' strategies going ahead to the 2028 presidential election.

#189. Andrew Scott drama Elsinore leads British independent film award nominations

Elsinore is in contention for 12 Bifas, including for Scott’s highly praised performance, while Clio Barnard’s I See Buildings Fall Like Lightning, Ashley Walters’ Animol and Mike Leigh’s Tender Loving Care are also recognised

Elsinore, the drama in which Andrew Scott plays the actor Ian Charleson as he takes on Shakespeare’s Hamlet in the last months of his life, before he died of Aids in 1990, has emerged as the leading contender for the British independent film awards (Bifas).

The film, which is directed by Simon Stone and co-stars Olivia Colman, Billie Piper and Johnny Flynn, received 12 nominations, including best British independent film, best director for Stone and best lead performance for Scott. Elsinore has collected rave reviews since its premiere at the Telluride film festival in September, with the Guardian’s Benjamin Lee describing it as “a devastating movie [that] is a reminder of Charleson’s strength and the many men like him and it implores us to remember them too”. Scott is also already being tipped as the favourite for the best actor Oscar – and he would be the first out gay actor to win.

In second place is Clio Barnard’s Birmingham-set ensemble drama I See Buildings Fall Like Lightning, which has eight nominations including best British independent film, best director for Barnard and best ensemble performance for its main cast. In a five-star review, the Guardian’s chief critic Peter Bradshaw said its “mix of poignancy, defiance and contaminated euphoria stayed with me hours after the closing credits”.

Animol, the feature-length directing debut of Top Boy and Adolescence star Ashley Walters, came in third with seven nominations, including best lead performance for Tut Nyuot and best debut director for Walters. Mike Leigh’s final film, Tender Loving Care, has four nominations.

With their focus on British independent cinema, the Bifas are also distinctive for their non-gendered acting categories, in which Scott and Nyuot will compete against, among others, Juliette Binoche for dementia drama Queen at Sea and Leisa Gwenllian for Welsh-language stage play adaptation Effi o Blaenau.

The Bifas have an award for cinema of the year which was won in its inaugural year in 2025 by the Magic Lantern Cinema in Tywyn, Wales. This year’s contenders include Campus West in Welwyn Garden City, the Hippodrome in Bo’ness, and the Ultimate Picture Palace in Oxford, the last of which is is threatened with closure by its landlord, Oriel College, Oxford University.

The winners will be announced at a ceremony at the Roundhouse in London on 6 December.

#187. Dogecoin jumps after Elon Musk replaces Twitter bird with Shiba Inu

Twitter’s traditional bird icon was booted and replaced with an image of a Shiba Inu, an apparent nod to dogecoin, the joke cryptocurrency that CEO Elon Musk is being sued over.

Musk addressed the change Monday afternoon, tweeting, “as promised” above an image of a year-old conversation in which another user suggested that Musk “just buy Twitter” and “change the bird logo to a doge.”

Elon Musk's Twitter promised a purge of blue check marks. Instead he singled out one account

The doge logo appeared on the site two days after Musk asked a judge to throw out a $258 billion racketeering lawsuit accusing him of running a pyramid scheme to support the dogecoin, according to Reuters.

Lawyers for Musk and Tesla called the lawsuit by dogecoin investors a “fanciful work of fiction” over Musk’s “innocuous and often silly tweets.”

It wasn’t clear whether the logo change was permanent. Musk has been known to use Twitter to troll both his fans and his critics.

The price of dogecoin, which is typically volatile, was up more than 20% over the past 24 hours, to about 9 cents. It was trading just under 8 cents Monday morning.

Dogecoin was created December 6, 2013, by a pair of software engineers — as a joke. The name is a nod to the “doge” meme that became popular a decade ago. Its Shiba Inu mascot mimicks that meme: a dog surrounded by a bunch of Comic Sans text in broken English.

#184. Rent rises of up to 88% still passing in England despite Renters’ Reform Act

Exclusive: While tenants are challenging increases in record numbers, renters and landlords say system is ‘not working for anyone’

The rent tribunal system is “not working for anyone”, tenants and landlords have said, as rent increases of up to 88% continue to be approved despite Labour’s pledge to curb unfair rises.

Tenants are challenging rent increases in record numbers since the Renters’ Reform Act came into effect in May, making the process easier and less risky for renters.

But tenants and landlords have said people are facing increasingly long delays for hearings, and that there is a lack of consistency in how “fair market rent” is being decided.

Analysis of data from May to October by London Renters Union (LRU) showed 80% of tenants who went through the tribunal process saw their rent go up, by an average of £1,200 a year, although in most cases it was less than the landlord’s proposed increase.

In 36% of cases, the landlord’s proposed increase was approved in full, and in 42% of cases the rent increase was reduced. In 16% of cases the rent increase was cancelled, and in 6% rent was lowered.

In one case, a rent tribunal approved an 88% rent increase on a property in Dagenham. The tenant, who paid rent via housing benefit, argued the increase would lead to undue hardship but his monthly rent still went up from £1,200 to £2,250.

In another case, a tenant who challenged a £17-a-month rent increase received a £200 rent decrease after the tribunal ruled the rent was actually too high.

Tenants’ unions have expressed concern over rent increases of 30% or more being regularly approved.

Jae Vail from LRU said: “The system is just clearly not doing what Labour said it would do in their manifesto, which was helping people challenge unreasonable rent increases. What could be a better definition of an unreasonable rent increase than 40% or 80%?

“And in places where rents in an entire area are rising really rapidly, this doesn’t protect you because it’s quite hard to evidence this is actually out of character.”

He added that renters were starting to face long delays to have their cases heard, and that tenants in London had been asked to attend hearings in the Midlands due to backlogs.

The changes, which took effect in May, mean the tribunal can no longer increase rent higher than the landlord’s application, and rent increases can no longer be backdated to before the tribunal decision. The abolition of “no fault” evictions also reduced the risk of landlords retaliating against tenants. The cost of applying is £47.

Chris Norris, chief policy officer at the National Residential Landlords Association, said the system “wasn’t working for anyone” and there were concerns that inaccurate decisions were being made by an overstretched workforce.

“It’s very difficult for landlords to trust and have faith in the system,” he said. “There hasn’t been any more capacity or resource put into the tribunal system by the government to make sure this works, and only just over 30 [full-time] judges across the country are working on this.

“We’ve seen judgments based pretty much exclusively on a trawl of Rightmove or similar sites, which is not that helpful.”

He also said there were concerns tenants’ unions were encouraging members to challenge all rent increases, which he described as an “abuse of the system”.

Oxford Renters Union has asked its members to pledge to say no to rent increases by taking them to tribunal, with the goal of “freezing rents and lowering the market rate for all Oxford renters”.

“We need to make sure bad landlords don’t try to replicate no-fault evictions by using rent increases. It’s absolutely essential,” Norris said. “But unfortunately, it stops serving that purpose if you deliberately try to gum up the system.”

But unions pushed back on this, saying there was no evidence to suggest renters were “flooding the system”.

Nye Jones from Generation Rent said the tribunal system had been working well since May but further change was needed. “It is based on what’s deemed a fair market rent in the current market. But we know that in many places, renters are already paying way, way more than they can afford on rent. We need to look at how we can reduce the relative cost of rent over time,” he said.

Renters’ groups are calling on the government to set controls on rent increases across the country to make renting more affordable while housing stock steadily increases.

Vail said: “What we’re seeing right now is a really inefficient case-by-case form of rent control. So why not have the version that would actually protect everyone?”

A spokesperson for the Ministry of Housing, Communities and Local Government said: “Our Renters’ Rights Act empowers tenants to challenge above-market rent increases through the tribunal – stopping private landlords from using increases as a backdoor means of eviction.

“We’re already seeing evidence that our changes are working – with cases across the country where tribunals have reduced above-market rents by hundreds of pounds a month or ruled there should be no increase.”

Kim Yo Jong dismissed FBI claims that North Korean hackers stole more than $6 billion from Binance, as the exchange began reopening withdrawals on more networks.

#182. Erdogan's political fate may be determined by Turkey's Kurds

Editor’s Note:  A version of this story first appeared in CNN’s Meanwhile in the Middle East newsletter, a three-times-a-week look inside the region’s biggest stories. Sign up here.

Turkey’s persecuted pro-Kurdish party has emerged as a kingmaker in the country’s upcoming election, playing a decisive role that may just tip the balance enough to unseat two-decade ruler Recep Tayyip Erdogan.

In a key setback to the Turkish president and leader of the Justice and Development Party (AK Party), the pro-Kurdish Peoples’ Democratic Party (HDP) last month announced that it would not put forward its own presidential candidate, a move analysts say allows its supporters to vote for Erdogan’s main rival.

“We are facing a turning point that will shape the future of Turkey and (its) society,” said the HDP in a statement on March 23. “To fulfill our historical responsibility against the one-man rule, we will not field a presidential candidate in (the) May 14 elections.”

It is a twist of irony for the Turkish strongman, who spent the better half of the past decade cracking down on the party after it began chipping away at his voter base. Its former leader Selahattin Demirtas has been in prison for nearly seven years and the party faces possible closure by a court for suspected collusion with the militant Kurdistan Workers’ Party (PKK) and affiliated groups. But its influence may nonetheless determine the course of Turkey’s politics.

The HDP’s decision not to field a candidate came just three days after head of the Republican People’s Party (CHP) Kemal Kilicdaroglu, Erdogan’s main rival, visited the party’s co-chairs. He told reporters that the solution to Turkey’s problems, “including the Kurdish problem” lies in parliament,” according to Turkish media.

Kilicdaroglu, who represents the six-party Nation Alliance opposition bloc, is the strongest contender to run against Erdogan in years. And while the HDP hasn’t yet announced whether it will put its weight behind him, analysts say it is the kingmaker in the elections.

Erdogan's 'polar opposite' wants to replace him as president of Turkey

“It was a carefully crafted political discourse,” Hisyar Ozsoy, deputy co-chair of the HDP and a member of parliament from the predominantly Kurdish province of Diyarbakir, told CNN. “We are not going to have our own candidate, and we will leave it to the international community to interpret it the way they wish.”

Experts say the crackdown on the HDP is rooted in the threat it poses to Erdogan politically, as well as its position as one of the main parties representing Turkey’s Kurds, an ethnic minority from which a separatist militant movement has emerged.

The party and the Kurdish people have had a complicated relationship with Erdogan. The leader courted the Kurds in earlier years by granting them more rights and reversing restrictions on the use of their language. Relations with the HDP were also cordial once, as Erdogan worked with the party on a brief peace process with the PKK.

But ties between Erdogan and the HDP later turned sour, and the HDP fell under a sweeping crackdown aimed at the PKK and their affiliates.

Kurds are the biggest minority in Turkey, making up between 15% and 20% of the population, according to Minority Rights Group International.

It is unclear if the HDP will endorse Kilicdaroglu, but analysts say that the deliberate distance may be beneficial for the opposition candidate.

The accusations against the HDP place it in a precarious position during the elections. It currently faces a case in Turkey’s Constitutional Court over suspected ties to the PKK, which is designated as a terrorist group by Turkey, the United States and the European Union. Knowing it may be banned at any moment, its candidates are running under the Green Left Party in parliament.

If the opposition is seen as allying with the HDP, Erdogan’s AK Party may use its influence in the media to discredit it as being pro-PKK, said Murat Somer, a political science professor at Koc University in Istanbul and author of Return to Point Zero, a book on the Turkish-Kurdish question in Turkey.

The HDP’s threat to Erdogan’s hold on power became apparent after the June 2015 election, the first general election it participated in. It won 13% of the seats, denying the ruling AK Party its majority for the first time since 2002. Erdogan, however, called a snap election five months later, which led to a drop in the HDP’s support to 10.7%, as well as the restoration of the AK Party’s overall majority.

“They are a kingmaker in these elections because the HDP gets about half of the votes of the Kurdish population in Turkey,” said Somer, adding that the other, more conservative Kurdish voters have traditionally voted for Erdogan’s AK Party. And last month, the Free Cause Party (HUDA-PAR), a tiny Kurdish-Islamist party announced support for Erdogan in the elections. The party has never won seats in parliament.

Erdogan's political fate may rest on his response to the earthquake

The HDP knows that its position is key to the outcome of next month’s vote, but that it’s also in a delicate situation.

“We want to play the game wisely, and we need to be very careful,” said Ozsoy, adding that the party wants to avoid a “contaminated political climate” where the elections are polarized “between a very ugly ultra-nationalist discourse against Kilicdaroglu and others.”

The party was founded in 2012 with a number of aims, said Ozsoy, one of which was “peaceful and democratic resolution of the Kurdish conflict.”

Somer said that the party was seen to be “an initiative” of the PKK, which later led to a heavy government crackdown on it in the name of counterterrorism.

Its former leader Demirtas remains an influential figure.

The Turkish government has been trying to link the HDP to the PKK but has so far failed to prove “a real connection,” said Asli Aydintasbas, a visiting fellow at the Brookings Institution in Washington, DC.

A post-Erdogan Turkey may give some breathing space to the Kurds and Kurdish-dominated parties in Turkey, Aydintasbas told CNN, noting that many Kurdish voters have recently left Erdogan’s camp. “For HDP, this is more than just an ideological choice,” she said. “It’s a matter of survival.”

Ozsoy says his party understands what’s at stake, not only for Turkey’s Kurds but for all its minorities.

“We are aware of our responsibility here. We are aware of our role. We know we are in a kingmaker position,” the HDP lawmaker said.

#178. Israeli military veterans, a backbone of protest movement, vow to keep demonstrating

In a sea of Israeli flags, Yiftach Golov holds one that looks a little different.

Among the hundreds of thousands of protesters who took to the streets for the 13th week in a row on Saturday, Golov hoists a brown flag that represents a group called “Brother and Sisters in Arms.”

They are veterans – many, like Golov, from elite forces – who now feel they are fighting on a new battlefield: To save Israeli democracy.

“We believe this is our responsibility to go once again called to the flag of the nation to stop this madness to defend Israel,” Golov said, as he weaved his way through the protesters on Tel Aviv’s Kaplan street, between the high-rises that house many of Israel’s high tech companies.

During the second intifada, in the early 2000s, Golov served in a special forces reconnaissance unit. He was never before particularly political, focusing more on getting his PhD in biophysics from Tel Aviv University.

But when the protest movement against the Israeli government’s judicial overhaul plan began in January, Golov attended one a demonstration and soon became one of thousands of veterans, and now military reservists, who have taken up the cause as their new mission.

Some, including elite Air Force reservists, have taken it a step further, threatening not to heed the call to train or even serve in protest of the government’s plans planned judicial changes, which would give the governing parties more control over Israel’s judiciary.

Others have taken to becoming some of the most active organizers and demonstrators. Last week, a group from Brothers and Sisters in Arms protested by carrying a figure wrapped in the Israeli flag on a stretcher, the way they would carry a wounded comrade off the field.

While Golov says he has not taken the drastic step to refuse service, he understands the motivation.

“We’re fighting for justice and liberty, just like the American story, that’s the values that that are being represented symbolized back when we look at our flag, that’s something that was lacking lacking for the last few decades. So basically, we reclaim the flag,” he said.

Palestinian taxi driver recounts surviving attack by right-wing protesters

Fellow members of the group, all wearing brown shirts with the organization’s logo, come up and say hello. They’re sprinkled all throughout the protests. One is even leading the “Pink Front,” a group of coordinated drummers who look like they are dressed for a rave, and often lead the chants at the protests.

They’re using skills they learned in the military – how to organize, how to mobilize – now for the protests. But more importantly, they say they have the same type of motivation.

“The very deep feeling that you are part of something bigger than yourself, that (you’re) allowed to sacrifice anything that is needed, whether it’s your career, health, seriously mental health,” Golov said. “We all have a mission, you’re willing to do it at any cost. You’re very determined, you know that you are on the right side, you’re carrying the torch of light. That keeps us being highly motivated despite the fact that we’re not sleeping for days.”

Israel’s protest movement is made up of many disparate groups, but the pressure from Israel’s much vaunted veterans has been seen as a key to moving the needle.

Last Monday, after weeks of sustained protests and the largest general strike in Israeli history, Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu announced a pause to the legislation, to allow time for negotiations with the opposition.

But despite the announcements, protesters are still out in the streets in large numbers. CNN affiliate Channel 12 in Israel estimated the size of Saturday’s demonstration in Tel Aviv at about 150,000 people. Organizers claimed it was 230,000.

Last week’s mass protests and widespread strike action came after Netanyahu said he had decided to fire Defense Minister Yoav Gallant for advocating a delay in passing the legislation – a move that Netanyahu has since delayed, sources told CNN, due to “the present security situation.”

In his televised speech calling for a delay, Gallant had said the pause in the legislation was needed “for the security of Israel,” citing the refusal of some Israel Defense Forces reservists to train in protest of the government plans. He said pressing ahead with the proposals could threaten Israel’s security.

Under pressure at home and from allies abroad, Netanyahu said he would delay votes on the remaining legislation until after the Knesset’s Passover recess in April “to give time for a real chance for a real debate.”

“Out of the responsibility to the nation, I decided to delay … the vote, in order to give time for discussion,” he added.

But Netanyahu indicated that the delay was only temporary. He insisted that the overhaul was necessary, and reiterated criticism of refusal to train or serve in the military in protest at the planned changes. “Refusing is the end of our country,” he said.

Many protesters don’t believe that the pause is real, or say it’s simply a stalling tactic to give Netanyahu some breathing room and get the protesters to go home before he plows on with the reforms.

“We will start doing deactivation only when we will know 100% that Israel state will stay a functional democratic country. Whatever needs to be done for that,” Golov said.

#177. New tool will help gardeners move away from thirsty English classics to drought-resistant plants

Exclusive: Garden Climate Assessment Tool will allow users to predict a plant’s response to the projected climate in a given location

“In the 20th century, we could pretty much grow the same things with confidence, we could learn from previous generations,” said Prof Sam Brockington, a curator at Cambridge University Botanic Garden.

“But now we’re moving into climate scenarios that we just don’t have the traditions to understand fully.”

As gardeners around the UK are forced to adapt their growing habits to a rapidly changing climate, a coalition of leading UK horticultural organisations is developing an online tool that will help users predict which plants would survive in future conditions.

The Garden Climate Assessment Tool (GCAT), developed by the Future National Gardens Group (FNGG), will be a free risk-assessment tool that evaluates how a plant could tolerate the projected climate of a user’s chosen location.

Gardeners will be able to feed in a list of plants into the tool, which will take into account a number of factors including annual average temperature, natural distribution, winter cold and climate dryness.

The FNGG comprises horticultural, heritage and landscape organisations including the Royal Horticultural Society (RHS), the National Trust, English Heritage and the Royal Botanic Gardens.

On Monday, professional gardeners were invited to trial and provide feedback on the tool. A timeline for its release is uncertain, but the RHS’s director of science, Alistair Griffiths, said the long-term hope was that it would be made accessible to the wider gardening public.

It was created partly to spur the creation of climate-resilient gardens in the UK, and to inform gardeners’ decisions about what they plant in the future, aiming to help them anticipate the effects of the climate crisis on their plants rather than reacting to them.

Brockington said the new tool would identify plants in the UK that are struggling, so gardeners would be able to gradually remove them from collections.

The GCAT was based on Botanic Gardens Conservation International’s climate assessment tool, which was launched in Australia in 2022, focusing solely on trees. The UK coalition sought to move the model forward by expanding it to cover plants. There are up to 80,650 plant types and data from 289 UK gardens currently in the system, with plans for it to be continually updated to help strengthen predictions.

UK gardeners are increasingly having to rethink what they plant – drought is still declared in most of England, while rolling heatwaves scorched gardens and parks during the summer.

Laura Carey, who co-owns Carey Garden Design Studio with her husband, Joe, said: “One of the big questions for designers at the moment are what trees are going to be able to cope with our changing weather … Things like silver birch, I would be really wary of planting that in a garden, unless I knew that it was a fairly moist environment for it, because we’ve just seen them suffer so much over the past two very hot summers.”

She said the tool’s ability to measure how a plant would fare “not just with increased temperatures, but also with the changeability of the weather” could help gardeners make well-rounded and longer-term choices.

The planet's most important stories. Get all the week's environment news - the good, the bad and the essential

Clive Boase, of the charity Climate Change Gardening, said he welcomed the tool, at a time when people were “hungry for information on how to help their garden cope with the changing climate”.

He said it could help gardeners in his area in the east of England – the driest region in the UK – become more confident in moving away from classic features of the English garden that require a lot of water, such as verdant lawns and flowers including hydrangeas and delphiniums.

“People are embedded in a sort of cultural aesthetic … And if you say, to be honest, that’s not going to work going forward, then that’s a bit of a wrench for them,” he said. “They have to be supported in making those changes.”

Griffiths said soil type was not currently a variable in the too. Kim Shrosbree, the secretary of the Epsom Garden Society, said it would be useful to consider how gardeners on chalk and clay were facing opposite growing conditions.

“If I search for my location in the tool and they gave me lots of plants that were suitable for the Epsom area, I could guarantee probably that 70% of those plants wouldn’t grow where I am because I’m on chalk,” she said. “To me, that would be just as important to know as the plants that will be good for the climate proposed in the south of England.”

Juliet Sargeant, a garden designer, said the tool’s location feature was essential, as gardeners increasingly need tailored advice. “Everybody really needs to think more about individual plants and your own garden your own location,” she said. “I think in the past we’ve been able to get away with quite generic advice. But now things are going to depend very much on your own garden.”

#174. India news: Cyclone Murasu wreaks havoc after landfall

Cyclone Murasu made landfall in Andhra Pradesh, leaving a trail of destruction. Weather agencies have warned of heavy rainfall in the next 12 hours. Follow DW for more.

Here is a roundup of the biggest headlines from across India on Tuesday, November 3.

That is all for today. We will be back with more news from India tomorrow.

The National Democratic Alliance (NDA) is seemingly set for a sweeping victory in Andhra Pradesh municipal elections that were held in 92 urban districts across the state.

The elections are an important contest between the ruling NDA coalition, comprising the Telugu Desam Party, Jana Sena Party and the Bharatiya Janata Party, and the Opposition Yuvajana Sramika Rythu Congress Party (YSRCP), often shortened to simply the YSR Congress Party.

The NDA nominees are leading in almost all municipal corporations and a majority of municipalities in the state.

The NDA is expected to win more than 90% of the wards/divisions in urban civic bodies.

The state's civic poll is being seen as a political test for the NDA led by Chief Minister N Chandrababu Naidu. The result of the civic polls is also expected to offer an indication of the YSR Congress Party's popularity in the state's urban areas.

More than 4,000 people were evicted in the northeastern state of Assam after authorities demolished homes during an eviction drive, officials told Indian media outlet Scroll.

The demolition drive took place in Assam's Dhubri district's Alamganj Part-IX village. Local media reported that most of the 4,000 displaced people were Bengali Muslims.

Citing Dhubri District Commissioner Dibakar Nath, Scroll reported that eviction notices had been served to residents occupying 500 acres of land. Authorities evicted the families, claiming it was government land under encroachment.

Nath claimed that residents had settled on the government land in different phases, with some arriving five years ago and others a decade ago. "Most of them have land in other places," he alleged.

However, residents have disputed this claim. Several families reportedly had been paying land revenue for years.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Over the past few years, eviction drives have become more frequent in Assam, under the Hindu-nationalist Bharatiya Janata Party-led government.

In the last ten years of BJP rule in Assam, around 19,000 families, the majority of them Muslim, have been evicted from government land, according to data from the state revenue and disaster management department and the district authorities compiled by Scroll.

Union Home Minister Amit Shah on Monday claimed that 30 million of the 130 million voters removed from the electoral rolls during the mass voter roll revisions were "Bangladeshi infiltrators," without offering further evidence.

Speaking at a rally in Hyderabad, Shah said, "Thirty million votes were those who never existed," Shah claimed. "These votes were those of Bangladeshi infiltrators…Tell me whether these votes should be cut or not?"

Doubling down on his attack on the opposition alliance INDIA bloc, Shah said the INDIA bloc rally held in Delhi on Sunday was not to save votes but to save infiltrators who form the vote bank of "Congress and company."

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

"The Congress party wants to capture elections through bogus voting," Shah alleged.

The opposition in India has been pushing back against the mass voter deletions and demanding the resignation of election chief Gyanesh Kumar, alleging his role in deliberate voter deletions favoring Shah's Bharatiya Janata Party.

The Indian Navy coordinated a 48-hour operation and rescued 20 crew members from the Iranian fishing vessel Azini after it was hijacked by armed pirates off the coast of Somalia.

In response, the Indian Navy tasked a mission-deployed warship to intervene, supported by a maritime reconnaissance aircraft.

The vessel was seized by Somali pirates on October 29.

According to officials, the operation was carried out 3,000 km from Mumbai in the Arabian Sea.

"Executing a swift and well-coordinated tactical intervention, the Indian Navy secured the vessel, overcame the pirate threat and ensured the safe release of all 20 crew members," the Navy said.

Iran thanked the Indian government for rescuing the crew.

"The Embassy of the Islamic Republic of Iran in New Delhi expresses its sincere appreciation to the Government of India, the Indian Navy and the Indian Air Force for their successful operation to rescue the 20 Iranian crew members of the fishing vessel Azini from pirates," the Iranian mission said in a post on social media.

An Indian tourist was killed and three others were injured after two helicopters crashed into each other while landing in Nepal's Solukhumbu district on Tuesday morning.

According to the Hindustan Times, which cited local officials, the Indian tourist was from Tamil Nadu's Chennai and identified as Vasanth Kumar Balaramaramadu (47).

Nepalese pilots Rabindra Prajapati of Fishtail Air and Prajwal KC of Dynasty Air were also injured in the incident and have been taken to Kathmandu for further treatment.

The helicopters were ferrying trekkers who had been hiking up to the Mount Everest base camp during Nepal's popular trekking season.

Reportedly, the incident took place at Pheriche village, which is at 4,200 meters (13,780 feet) altitude.

Residents in the Maharashtrian city of Karjat, which lies on the west coast, are pushing back against the large-scale tree felling for a proposed 3,000-MW pumped storage power plant in the region.

More than 32,000 trees are expected to be cleared for construction. According to a report by the Indian Express, nearly 1,100 trees were cut down before the operation was halted last week amid protests.

On Monday, a demonstration by residents, political groups, and environmental activists prompted an almost total shutdown of activity in Karjat.

The hydropower project is being built by Torrent Power is expected to be operational by 2029.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

The project needs 377 hectares of land along the Sahyadri range in Raigad district and is expected to cut Maharashtra’s dependence on coal-fired power.

However, environmental experts have warned against the clearing of the forest on the west coast, warning it will disrupt the moisture cycles that bring rain to regions far away.

Severe cyclonic storm 'Murasu' began its landfall close to Ongole in the Prakasam district of the coastal state of Andhra Pradesh during the early hours on Tuesday.

The cyclone, which, according to the Indian Meteorological Department (IMD), is currently moving towards the north-northwest, has left a trail of destruction in the region.

Packing strong winds with heavy rains, the cyclone reportedly has uprooted thousands of trees and damaged electric poles and crops in the coastal areas of Andhra Pradesh.

Schools and colleges have been closed in at least eight districts in Andhra Pradesh as a precautionary measure in view of the heavy rainfall and strong winds.

Heavy spells of rainfall were also likely across south coastal Andhra Pradesh during the next 12 hours, IMD said.

The cyclone is currently over the west central Bay of Bengal close to the south Andhra Pradesh coast and is likely to weaken gradually into a cyclonic storm by noon and further into a deep depression by Tuesday midnight.
