#141. Negotiators ‘braced for worst’ over US-Iran talks to reopen strait of Hormuz

Third day of indirect talks as Qatar tries to negotiate release of $6bn in Iranian assets that could lead to waterway’s reopening

Officials from the Gulf states are “braced for the worst” as they wait for Washington’s response to an Iranian proposal that could reopen the strait of Hormuz before the US midterm elections on Tuesday.

Indirect talks in Doha ran into a third day on Sunday with Qatar trying to negotiate the release of more than $6bn in Iranian assets, which could lead to the reopening of the strait of Hormuz and the restart of negotiations on Iran’s nuclear programme.

The United Arab Emirates envoy Anwar Gargash said another round of escalation was not in the interests of Iran or any of its neighbours. He said: “We are also, as we have shown before, braced for the worst.”

He added: “Relations with Iran are essential, because Iran is our neighbour and we do not want to see it get caught up in a prolonged crisis.”

Qatar’s mediation continued despite unidentified missiles striking a Pakistan-bound Qatari LNG tanker near the strait of Hormuz on Friday. The attack came despite the ship receiving direct clearance and safe-passage approval from Tehran just hours earlier.

In a bid to overcome the lack of trust on both sides, Qatar has been focusing its efforts on reaching an agreement for a plan to compress the process by which the US lifts sanctions and then Iran reopens the strait of Hormuz and starts nuclear talks. Iran has agreed to cut the timetable from seven to two days.

Iran’s foreign minister, Abbas Araghchi, said he hoped the US would choose “diplomacy over other paths”. He said Iran was ready for both defence and diplomacy, depending on Washington’s “rationality”. Araghchi said there were currently no plans for direct talks with Washington.

Araghchi has not been attending the mediation in Doha and is due to go to Turkmenistan on Monday, but other Iranian diplomats are heavily involved.

Donald Trump said on Saturday the US had knocked out much of Iran’s military but still had to “take care of” one or two targets, as Tehran warned of reprisals if attacked. He maintained that negotiations with Iran to end the war, started by the US and Israel in February, were “going fine but you know they’re not very reliable”.

Earlier on Saturday, the US secretary of state, Marco Rubio, said the US was open to a diplomatic solution, while Iran’s security chief, Mohsen Rezaei, disclosed that he had received a letter from the US vice-president, JD Vance, seeking to revive talks.

Rezaei noted what he described as a positive change in “tone” from the US side but said he had yet to respond.

Speaking to Al Jazeera on Saturday, Araghchi said Iran had offered to open the strait of Hormuz within two days of the US acting to lift the blockade on oil ships, stop blocking the release of Iranian assets and end sanctions on Iranian oil exports.

Ali Nikzad, the deputy speaker of Iran’s parliament, once again signalled that Tehran would be unwilling to discuss its nuclear programme, saying: “Our enemies must understand that the Iranian nation has made a decision not to negotiate on the nuclear issue under any circumstances or under any pressure. This great nation gave its enemies enough opportunity to negotiate in this area, but was faced with repeated breaches of promises and illegal bombing of part of peaceful nuclear facilities.”

Rezaei claimed Trump was being hounded by a war that he claimed was having wider international repercussions than the Vietnam war. He told Iranian TV on Saturday: “Today, wherever Trump goes, he must answer why he started the war against Iran. There is no family in America that has not endured the pressure caused by the war in Iran.”

#139. Asian Cup postponed due to Middle East conflict

The Asian Cup has become the latest sporting event to be affected by the ongoing conflict in the Middle East. Organizers cite "exceptional circumstances" in Saudi Arabia, which has seen several Houthi attacks of late.

A little over two months before it was set to kick off, the Asian Football Confederation (AFC) has postponed its flagship international football tournament in the wake of recent deadly attacks on host country, Saudi Arabia.

The AFC cited "substantial logistical complexities and travel disruptions" caused by conflicts in the region.

Instead of January 7, 2027, the tournament will now kick off on December 23 and end on January 21, 2028. The postponement follows delays and cancellations of a number of sporting events in the region, including a Formula One race, the men's and women's tennis finals, the 2027 Dakar Rally and disruption to Asia's marquee continental club football tournament, the AFC Champions League Elite.

Saudi Arabia hosted, and won, the Gulf Cup last month, but there had already been much talk about whether the much larger Asian Cup could go ahead.

It was scheduled to feature 24 teams playing matches in eight venues across Riyadh, Jeddah and Khobar. However, the AFC said that "circumstances arising from developments in the region, including substantial logistical complexities and travel disruptions," made the tournament impossible.

"Exceptional circumstances have been created for the organization and delivery of the competition as originally scheduled, making the postponement necessary in the interest of all participating teams, officials, spectators and other stakeholders."

In particular, the postponement was forced by a recent wave of deadly attacks on Saudi Arabia by Iran-backed Houthi rebels as well as other raging conflicts in the region. The Houthis have targeted Saudi infrastructure, including airports. They are fighting a Saudi-led coalition supporting Yemen's internationally recognized government, a civil war that reignited in July following a years-long truce.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

The AFC had been under pressure from several federations to postpone the tournament, with South Korea's players leading the way.

"The ongoing military tensions and security instability in the Middle East are issues that cannot be taken lightly," Lee Keun-ho, president of the Korea Professional Footballers Association, said last month, according to the Yonhap News Agency.

Lee added that "no tournament's value can take precedence over the lives and safety of players and football fans."

It seems that the AFC now agrees. However, following the announcement of the postponement, Yasser al-Misehal, chairman of the local organizing committee, promised that Saudi Arabia was "fully ready" whenever the tournament ended up being held.

"The tournament will move to the new dates, and our preparations will continue as planned," he told the Saudi Press Agency.

"We're working closely with the AFC and all our partners to make sure the tournament runs smoothly and meets the high standards expected of Saudi Arabia and the Asian Cup."

Saudi Arabia has invested hugely in sports over the last decade, from owning Premier League teams to hosting boxing title fights and bidding for a number of major sporting events, most notably World Cup 2034, which they won the right to host unopposed.

However, the country has recently withdrawn or slashed funding for a number of sporting events including golf, rugby, tennis and winter sports.

#138. Israel has ‘historic’ opportunity to change direction, says leader of Arab coalition

Yousef Jabareen says he is ready to join forces with opposition bloc to oust Benjamin Netanyahu after winning record number of seats in Knesset

The leader of Israel’s main Palestinian coalition, which holds the balance of power after Tuesday’s elections, has said the country is facing a historic opportunity to change direction.

Yousef Jabareen, the head of the Joint List, said he was ready to join forces with the “Change” bloc to oust Benjamin Netanyahu and his far-right coalition, and to wrest control of the Knesset.

Turnout among Arab Israelis (Palestinians with Israeli citizenship, 20% of the population) more than doubled to about 80%, transforming the political landscape. Palestinians in the occupied West Bank and Gaza had no vote.

Jabareen said the first demand of the roughly 840,000 Palestinian Israeli voters was to get rid of the Netanyahu government, and in particular its most extreme members, Itamar Ben-Gvir, the national security minister, and Bezalel Smotrich, the finance minister.

“This has been an awful four years … in all areas of life: in terms of house demolition, criminal gangs within the Arab community without any [intervention] from the police; not allocating the needed resources for our municipalities, for our education, for our youth in general; racism in all areas of life – physical attacks against Arabs, the brutality of the police,” Jabareen told the Guardian in one of his first interviews since the election. “So this has been really a very dangerous period.”

He said the next few days would be critical. Israel’s president, Isaac Herzog, is due to summon political leaders as early as Wednesday to canvass their recommendations on who should be given the first chance to form the next government, with no single grouping commanding a clear claim on power.

Netanyahu’s coalition lost its majority, dropping from 68 to 53 seats in the 120-seat Knesset. The Change bloc of Zionist parties led by the retired general Gadi Eisenkot has 48 seats, leaving the Arab-Israeli parties as potential kingmakers with a record 19 seats. The Joint List has 11, while the more conservative, Islamist Ra’am has eight.

Eisenkot and his partners have pledged not to form a unity government with Netanyahu, but Eisenkot has also put conditions on any potential coalition with Arab parties: they have to recognise that Israel is a Jewish state, accept compulsory national service – either civil or military – and denounce Hamas as a terrorist organisation.

Ra’am and its leader, Mansour Abbas, have signalled it is ready to join an Eisenkot coalition. The Joint List has no intention of joining such a government, but Jabareen said the coalition was ready to keep it afloat from the outside with tactical voting in the Knesset.

“The conditions that [Eisenkot] speaks of are more relevant for parties who would be in the coalition. We are not planning to be in the coalition … for very clear reasons,” Jabareen said at his family home in the northern city of Umm al-Fahm. “As long as the occupation of our people in the West Bank continues, we cannot be part of a government.

“However, in order to use our political power to get rid of the extreme Netanyahu government, we said that we will consider the possibility of supporting from outside. This is an opportunity to change policies, to change attitudes, including towards the Arab community and towards the Palestinian people.

“This is really a historic moment and I hope that there will be partners within the opposition to go ahead with that. We are thinking that this could be the start of a change.”

Jabareen, 54, a former law professor and the leader of the socialist Hadash party, has yet to talk to Eisenkot since Tuesday’s elections, but he confirmed that informal discussions among opposition parties were under way.

Forming a new government was unlikely to be straightforward. The party leaders in the Change bloc have to agree on a candidate for prime minister. They would also have to accept partnership with Arab parties, either in coalition or from outside. The rightwing opposition leader, Avigdor Lieberman, is said to be the most resistant. Joint List officials said it was unclear whether partnership with Palestinians really crossed Lieberman’s red lines, or whether he was just holding out for a top cabinet position.

Even before a new government could be formed, Jabareen argued, the 67 opponents of Netanyahu in the new Knesset could use their clear majority to wrest control of legislative power. “That would start by changing the speaker of the Knesset,” he said, adding that the opposition could also take control of the committee that oversees the legislative agenda.

“That could be very crucially important … even before we reach conclusions on the issue of a coalition and a new government,” he said, adding that one of the priorities of the new Knesset should be to repeal laws passed by the outgoing majority that infringed the independence of Israel’s judiciary.

To allow an opposition coalition to oust Netanyahu and function as a minority government, Jabareen made clear it would also have to address deep-seated inequality in Israeli society.

“We have suffered from systemic discrimination for years, so if Eisenkot speaks of change, I believe that the change should come also for our community. We need to be treated equally,” he said. “We also ask for a political horizon. We would like to see the war stopped and the resumption of negotiation with the Palestinian leadership. We would like to see a start of a change.”

The Joint List leader said he was concerned over what Netanyahu might do to keep his grip on power. The prime minister has already made allegations, without evidence, that the Arab parties had used Qatari funds illegally in the election campaign, which Jabareen dismissed as a bid to “delegitimise the political power of the Arab community” and “a dangerous incitement”.

One of Jabareen’s concerns was that the Israeli leader would join Donald Trump in the resumption of war on Iran as a pretext for putting off political transition.

“This is really a critical period,” he said. “There should be a clear voice from the opposition to go against any attempt by him to initiate war … or dictate a new military agenda in Israel.”

This article was amended on 2 November 2026. An earlier version said that one of Yashar party leader Gadi Eisenkot’s conditions for a potential coalition partnership with Arab parties was to “accept conscription”. In fact, the condition is to accept compulsory national service, which could be civil or military.

#133. Most Israelis think settlements policy should change to avoid more sanctions, poll finds

Survey finds UK government’s decision to impose trade ban was not a decisive factor in how Israelis voted in election

The UK government’s decision to impose a ban on trade in goods from illegal settlements was not decisive in how Israelis voted in their general election, but a majority think the Israeli government’s settlements policy should change if it is going to lead to further sanctions, a new poll of Israelis shows.

There had been fierce criticism of the UK foreign secretary Ed Miliband’s decision in September to impose a trade ban and wider sanctions on illegal settlements, with some claiming it would drive Israeli voters into the arms of Benjamin Netanyahu.

A survey last month by Israeli social research group aChord shows overall support for foreign sanctions on violent settlers in the West Bank, but there was little support for the UK’s ban on trade with goods from illegal settlements, with many fearing it would end up being extended across Israel.

The survey is unique in showing how much the tougher line on Israel adopted by Britain and some foreign states was affecting the thinking of Israeli voters, and the extent to which Israelis thought it was a response to Israel’s actions or born of hostility to Israel, or even antisemitism.

The survey showed that three-quarters of supporters of the bloc backing Netanyahu said UK sanctions and trade ban policies towards Israel did not affect their vote at all, and to the small extent that they changed anyone’s vote, they shifted voters both ways, leaving no detectable net effect.

Just over three-quarters of all those surveyed (76) – had heard of the UK government announcement on settlements, even if 18 % had only heard the broad headline. Four-fifths of those that were aware of the announcement knew that the policy applied to the West Bank, and not the whole of Israel.

Israel’s relations with the west were “important or very important” to 60% of the supporters of the Change bloc of parties backing Gadi Eisenkot in deciding their vote. But even then these relationships came fifth in importance in a proffered group of six motives for voting. Only 30% of supporters of the Netanyahu bloc said relations with the west mattered in the same way.

But 71% of voters for the opposition Change bloc said the Israeli government should adapt its settlements policy to avoid an escalation of sanctions. Only 14% of Netanyahu bloc voters agreed, most saying instead that the government should continue the policy “whatever the price”.

A plurality of the public – 50% – said the settlement and construction policy in the West Bank should be adjusted to avoid sanctions escalation and international isolation, with 71% of Eisenkot bloc supporters agreeing to this proposition. Support for a change in the policy is strongest among supporters of the Arab parties.

Our morning email breaks down the key stories of the day, telling you what’s happening and why it matters

The finding prompted Hannah Weisfeld, the director of the British-Jewish organisation Yachad, to claim the research “illustrates that the Israeli public certainly recognises that if there is not a policy shift in relation to settlement enterprise, the international community is likely to try to create an incentive to shift things from the outside. If anything, that highlights the importance of UK and other allies taking action that targets the occupation.”

The survey also showed that concerns about foreign sanctions are highest when voters feel they will suffer a high personal price. Only 36% of all those surveyed agreed that the settlement policy should continue even if sanctions on banks that finance construction in illegal settlements led either to higher interest rates, difficulties in transferring money abroad, or using credit cards abroad. The poll suggests that the more people feel they will be damaged personally, the more they oppose the settlement policy.

A majority of all Israelis surveyed (52%) support sanctions on individual violent settlers, so long as the sanctions are imposed only on those acting violently, but 70% of Netanyahu bloc supporters oppose foreign sanctions in all circumstances.

There is much less widespread support for the policy of banning the import of goods from the illegal settlement, with 63% of all those surveyed opposing the ban and 26% either supporting the measure outright, or saying they would if it were underlined that the ban would not apply to goods from outside settlements.

#131. French minister under fire for Playboy magazine cover

French government minister Marlene Schiappa has come under fire from members of her own party after appearing on the front cover of Playboy magazine.

Schiappa, who has been a government minister since 2017, appeared on the cover of the magazine to accompany a 12-page interview she did on women’s and LGBT rights. Schiappa, who is the current Minister for the Social Economy and French Associations, was photographed for the cover wearing a white dress.

Schiappa has been a long-time advocate for women’s rights and was appointed as the country’s first ever Gender Equality Minister in 2017. In this role, she successfully spearheaded a new sexual harassment law which allows for on the spot fines to be issued to men who catcall, harass or follow women on the street.

Her appearance has drawn criticism from political colleagues including French Prime Minister Elisabeth Borne.

Borne pulled up Schiappa over the cover, telling her it “wasn’t appropriate, especially during this period,” CNN affiliate BFMTV reported Saturday, citing a source close to the prime minister.

France is currently in the throes of a political and social crisis triggered by French President Emmanuel Macron’s move to push ahead with controversial pension reforms despite widespread public opposition.

“We are in the middle of a social crisis, there is the issue of policing, there are people between life and death, and I have the impression of being behind a smoke screen,” Sandrine Rousseau, Green Party politician and fellow women’s rights activist, told BFMTV Friday.

France wracked by more pension protests amid rising violence on the streets

French politician, Jean Luc Mélenchon who came third in the 2022 presidential elections criticized both Schiappa’s appearance and the decision by French President Emmanuel Macron to give an interview to children’s magazine, Pif Gadget, this week.

“In a country where the President expresses himself in Pif and his minister in Playboy, the problem would be the opposition. France is going off the rails,” Mélenchon tweeted Saturday.

Schiappa responded to her critics in a tweet on Saturday, saying: “Defending the right of women to have control of their bodies, that’s everywhere and all the time. In France, women are free. With all due respect to the detractors and hypocrites.”

French Interior Minister, Gérald Darmanin came to Schiappa’s defense during an interview with French news channel CNews on Sunday calling her a “woman of character.”

“I wanted to say that Marlene Schiappa is a courageous female politician who has her character and who has her style which is not mine, but I respect,” he remarked.

Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy has arrived in Berlin for talks with German Chancellor Friedrich Merz. Kyiv is seeking more air defense missiles and help keeping its energy system running through winter.

Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy arrived in Berlin on Tuesday for talks with German Chancellor Friedrich Merz on strengthening support for Ukraine.

Kyiv is seeking more air-defense hardware to intercept Russian ballistic missiles, as well as support for its energy sector ahead of winter. The visit comes a week before US-led talks with Ukraine and Russia in Abu Dhabi.

"The key issues are our diplomatic coordination — with important meetings ahead — strengthening our air defence, and supporting our energy sector," Zelenskyy wrote on X.

His visit came hours after Russia launched a large overnight attack on Ukraine using ballistic missiles, cruise missiles and drones. At least 73 people were injured in a strike on a hospital in the eastern city of Kharkiv, regional prosecutors said. They said 226 patients had to be transferred to other hospitals.

Ukraine needs interceptors for air defense systems such as the US-made Patriot. Germany, one of Ukraine's biggest military, financial and humanitarian supporters, says it has already supplied all the Patriot systems it can spare without weakening its own air defenses.

Zelenskyy told EU leaders in October that Ukraine would need an additional €70 billion ($78 billion) from European governments next year. Germany plans to host a summit of countries backing Ukraine in mid-November to discuss further support ahead of winter.

The visit coincides with the opening of the two-day Berlin Security Conference, which brings together European officials and defense experts. Russia's war in Ukraine is high on the agenda.

Police said about 3,400 additional officers were deployed for the visit and the conference, with reinforcements from across Germany.

US envoys are due to discuss security guarantees and reconstruction with European and NATO representatives in Brussels this week. Berlin expects European representatives to be in Abu Dhabi, though they are unlikely to have a seat at the negotiating table.

#127. Oil price falls amid hope of a breakthrough to end Iran war

Brent crude drops by more than 6% to $105.55 a barrel, the lowest level in more than two weeks

The oil price has fallen to its lowest level in more two weeks amid new hopes of a breakthrough to end the Iran war.

Brent crude, the international benchmark, dropped by more than 6.5% to as low as $105.55 a barrel on Monday, the lowest since 16 October, sending shares in airline companies higher and pushing down government borrowing costs.

Oil prices fell after Tehran revealed that contacts between Iran and the US to end their eight-month war were continuing.

“Consultations through mediators are very ⁠intensive. We have presented our conditions and ⁠proposals and everything depends on the political ​rationality of Washington,” the Iranian foreign minister, Abbas Araghchi, said, according to the state media outlet IRNA.

Over the weekend, Qatari officials tried to negotiate the release of more than $6bn (£4.5bn) in Iranian assets, so that Tehran might allow the strait of Hormuz to reopen.

On Monday, US vice-president JD Vance reported that there had been “a number of good conversations with the Iranians” and that he was feeling good about the prospects of a “negotiated settlement”.

Vance sent a letter to Iran’s security chief, Mohsen Rezaei, last week but the White House has offered few details about the contents of that correspondence.

Donald Trump had earlier taken a belligerent tone, telling voters in Iowa on Sunday that Iran would receive “the hammer” unless there was progress.

“The only reason why I’m not finished with Iran is I want to be nice. I want to try and save all those lives if I can ... But at some point we’re going to have to give them the hammer,” he said during a campaign rally in the run-up to the US midterm elections.

But investors were cheered by reports on Monday that Iran’s supreme leader, Mojtaba Khamenei, had granted “powers” to his government in its talks with the US seeking to end the Middle East war.

The drop in the oil price sparked hopes of a break in the inflationary pressures gripping the global economy. Government borrowing costs dropped, with the yield – or interest rate – on 10-year UK bonds down 10 basis points, or 0.1 of a percentage point, to the lowest since 22 September.

Strikes on eve of US midterms reportedly target surface-to-air missiles being used against US assets

The ⁠US launched limited strikes on ​Iran’s Qeshm Island late on Monday, hours after the vice-president, JD Vance, said he was optimistic that diplomatic efforts to end the war were moving in the right direction.

The strikes, first reported by the Wall Street Journal, came on the eve of crucial midterm elections in which Donald Trump’s Republican party is expected to be punished by voters for the cascading effect the war has had on the cost of living.

Unnamed US officials cited by the WSJ and other outlets described the attacks as defensive in nature.

Trump was asked earlier on Monday whether he had considered resuming strikes on Iran after Tuesday’s midterm elections and replied “no”.

Several explosions were ⁠heard ⁠on Qeshm ​Island on Monday evening, according to Iranian state media, with local outlets describing their cause as unclear.

The strikes targeted surface-to-air missiles being used against US assets, according to Reuters and the Associated Press.

US Central ‌Command did ​not immediately ⁠respond to ​a request for ​comment from the Reuters news agency, but the WSJ described the attacks as part of a series of strikes carried out against the country in recent weeks that had not been made public.

#123. The four astronauts NASA picked for the first crewed moon mission in 50 years

Sign up for CNN’s Wonder Theory science newsletter. Explore the universe with news on fascinating discoveries, scientific advancements and more.

Astronauts who will helm the first crewed moon mission in five decades were revealed on Monday, queuing up the quartet to begin training for the historic Artemis II lunar flyby that is set to take off in November 2024.

The astronauts are NASA’s Reid Wiseman, Victor Glover and Christina Koch, and Jeremy Hansen of the Canadian Space Agency.

Wiseman is a 47-year-old decorated naval aviator and test pilot who was first selected to be a NASA astronaut in 2009. A native of Baltimore, Maryland, he’s completed one prior spaceflight, a 165-day trip to the International Space Station that had launched aboard a Russian Soyuz rocket in 2014. Most recently, Wiseman served as chief of the astronaut office before stepping down in November 2022, making him eligible for a flight assignment.

Wiseman will serve as commander of the Artemis II mission.

Hansen, 47, is a fighter pilot who was selected by the Canadian Space Agency for astronaut training in 2009. From London, Ontario, Hansen is one of only four active Canadian astronauts, and he recently became the first Canadian to be put in charge of training for a new class of NASA astronauts.

He will be the first Canadian ever to travel to deep space.

Glover is a 46-year-old naval aviator who returned to Earth from his first spaceflight in 2021 after piloting the second crewed flight of SpaceX’s Crew Dragon spacecraft and spending nearly six months aboard the International Space Station.

“It’s so much more than the four names that have been announced,” Glover said during the Monday announcement at NASA Johnson Space Center in Houston. “We need to celebrate this moment in human history. … It is the next step in the journey that will get humanity to Mars.”

Glover, born in Pomona, California, served in several military squadrons in the United States and Japan in the 2000s, and he completed test pilot training with the US Air Force. When he was selected for the NASA astronaut corps in 2013, he was working in the US Senate as a legislative fellow. All told, Glover logged 3,000 flight hours in more than 40 aircraft, over 400 carrier arrested landings and 24 combat missions.

Glover’s first mission to space was as part of the SpaceX Crew-1 team, which launched to the International Space Station in November 2020 for a six-month stay on the orbiting laboratory.

Koch, 44, is a veteran of six spacewalks — including the first all-female spacewalk in 2019. She holds the record for the longest single spaceflight by a woman, with a total of 328 days in space. Koch is also an an electrical engineer who helped develop scientific instruments for multiple NASA mission. Koch, a native of Grand Rapids, Michigan, also spent a year at the South Pole, an arduous stay that could well prepare her for the intensity of a moon mission.

The Artemis II mission will build on Artemis I, an uncrewed test mission that sent NASA’s Orion capsule on a 1.4 million-mile voyage to lap the moon that concluded in December. The space agency deemed that mission a success and is still working to review all the data collected.

If all goes to plan, Artemis II will take off around November 2024. The crew members, strapped inside the Orion spacecraft, will launch atop a NASA-developed Space Launch System rocket from NASA’s Kennedy Space Center in Florida.

The journey is expected to last about 10 days and will send the crew out beyond the moon, potentially further than any human has traveled in history, though the exact distance is yet to be determined.

The “exact distance beyond the Moon will depend on the day of liftoff and the relative distance of the Moon from the Earth at the time of the mission,” NASA spokesperson Kathryn Hambleton said via email.

After circling the moon, the spacecraft will return to Earth for a splashdown landing in the Pacific Ocean.

Artemis II is expected to pave the way for the Artemis III mission later this decade, which NASA has vowed will put the first woman and person of color on the lunar surface. It will also mark the first time humans have touched down on the moon since the Apollo program ended in 1972.

The Artemis III mission is expected to take off later this decade. But much of the technology the mission will require, including spacesuits for walking on the moon and a lunar lander to ferry the astronauts to the moon’s surface, is still in development.

NASA is targeting a 2025 launch date for Artemis III, though the space agency’s inspector general has already said delays will likely push the mission to 2026 or later.

The space agency has been seeking to return people to the moon for more than a decade. The Artemis program was designed to pave the way to establishing a permanent lunar outpost, allowing astronauts to live and work deeper into space long term as NASA and its partners map a path to sending the first humans to Mars.

Vanessa Wyche, the director of NASA’s Johnson Space Center, declined to provide details to CNN about the selection process. But she emphasized the diversity of the Artemis II crew, which includes men and women rather than only a staff of White male test pilots as has been the case for historic missions of the past.

“I can tell you, they still all have the right stuff,” Wyche said. “We have requirements different than we did (when we) just had test pilots” on inaugural missions.

Koch said in an interview with CNN’s Ed Lavandera that the group found out they were selected a few weeks ago.

“We were all sent to a meeting that was on our calendars under a different pretext that didn’t sound as lofty as the one it was going to be,” Koch said. “And accidentally two of us were very late to that meeting.”

She said the offer rendered her “speechless.”

“It truly is an honor,” she added. “It’s an honor — not to get myself in the space — but because it’s amazing to be a part of this team that’s going back to the moon and on to Mars.”

An interview with the four astronauts will air on “CNN This Morning” on Tuesday, which starts at 6 am ET.

CNN’s Kristin Fisher contributed to this story.

#121. Who is hacking Europe’s supermarket fridges and freezers, and why?

After a second apparent cyber-attack in weeks, we examine possible culprits and their motives

Shop fridges and freezers across Europe hit by fresh suspected cyber-attack

Supermarket fridges and freezers in seven European countries have malfunctioned, their thermostats remotely cranked up in some cases to 50C (122F).

The EU has condemned what it said was “a deliberate attempt to disrupt our food supply chain and undermine consumers’ confidence in it”.

The attack hit fridges in Belgian branches of Carrefour, Intermarché and Ikea, Coop and ICA in Sweden, Delhaize in Luxembourg, Kesko in Finland, SuperValu in Ireland and shops in Estonia.

It appeared to be a reprise of attacks three weeks ago when French retailers had to ditch huge amounts of food when their fridges suddenly overheated as a result of anonymous hackers seizing control of their internet-exposed appliance management software.

Something similar may have happened in the US in early August when a series of mysterious fridge malfunctions hit commissaries at at least six military bases. The Pentagon is investigating a suspected hack.

This is feared to signal a new front in cyber-attack and hacking tactics. Fridges are not unique in having their controls exposed to hackers. Many ovens, washing machines, dishwashers and central heating systems are linked to the internet to allow remote access and automated control.

No politically motivated “hacktivist” group has claimed responsibility and neither has a ransomware gang exploited the vulnerability to demand the supermarkets pay – which in any event would be difficult because the damage is already done when their fridges overheat.

“The two most plausible attribution scenarios for me are some kind of state-based signalling and demonstration of capability or some kind of lone-wolf hacker that is conducting these attacks for their own personal reasons,” said Rafe Pilling, the senior director for threat intelligence at the cybersecurity company Sophos.

The state-linked actor theory seems most likely, in part because of the obvious psychological impact on large populations of seeing food stripped from shelves. North Korea is believed to have been behind last week’s Binance cryptocurrency heist, but in this case hackers linked to Russia and China are also likely to be in the frame.

Western intelligence chiefs say Moscow is waging a campaign of sabotage in Europe that takes many different forms and is likely to intensify in the coming months.

Discerning the culprit is made harder by the relative simplicity of the hack. No malware was needed, which means there is no trace of a weapon at the scene of the crime. Pilling compared it to “wearing rubber gloves and messing with the switches”.

The software that manages commercial fridges is connected to the internet, which means lines of code can be left vulnerable to hackers. Unlike desktop or mobile devices, on which users and IT managers are often alerted to the need to install updates that contain patches to repair constantly emerging vulnerabilities, the software in items such as fridges can be left for long periods without updates.

Nick Jackson, the director of cyber-operations at the cybersecurity company Bitdefender, said one reason companies might move slowly to install patches is they can take time to run, which leaves periods when machinery needs to be offline – an obvious problem for food retailers. But he said a simpler explanation could be that the people running the fridges are not aware of the problem, “don’t think about that and it’s not top of their agenda”.

In Monday’s wave of incidents, Coop in Sweden said some of its fridges were linked to a 4G mobile network that made them more exposed. ICA said its system was provided by Danfoss, the Danish company that had problems in France.

The simple answer is to patch their software systems. According to the Belgian refrigerator group Sabcobel, the impact of Monday’s hack could have been far worse if they had not started patching fridges after the 12 October attack in France.

In the UK, which has not yet been affected, retailers are “currently working with the National Cyber Security Centre(NCSC) to ensure their fridges and freezers are secure as possible against potential attacks” said Graham Wynn, the assistant director of regulatory affairs at the British Retail Consortium.

Internet-exposed refrigeration systems are most popular in countries including France, Italy, Greece and Belgium, according to the cybersecurity company Forescout.

Daniel dos Santos, its vice president of research, said: “When we checked the data for these exposed systems online of supermarkets, the UK is not in the top 10.” He said the risk was not negligible, but “I wouldn’t say it’s super-high right now for the UK specifically”.

A spokesperson for the NCSC said it was “working with its international partners and industry to fully understand details of an incident”.

#120. Football: Spanish player dies on after cardiac arrest on the pitch

Tenerife forward Jesus de Miguel has died following a collision with the opposing goalkeeper. The Spanish second division club has said it is "heartbroken" at the loss.

"Heartbroken with pain, with a lump in our throat and unable to believe this is real." Those were the words with which Spanish second division football club Tenerife confirmed the death of their 30-year-old player, Jesus de Miguel, on Tuesday.

De Miguel, 30, was involved in a clash in the middle of the pitch with FC Andorra's  Equatorial Guinea international goalkeeper Jesus Owono, who had raced out of his goal to try and clear the ball with the score 2-2 late in the game. Owono was sent off and the match was abandoned.

Spanish media said that De Miguel suffered a cardiac arrest and was resuscitated. The player was taken to hospital and placed in intensive care, with Tenerife later saying that De Miguel's condition had "worsened neurologically", before his death.

After confirming the death on Tuesday morning, the club posted that: "No words can ease the pain, nothing can lessen this great feeling of sadness. Our thoughts are with the family, his close friends and Tenerife fans, who today are grieving over this terrible news."

Andorra also sent their "most sincere condolences" as messages of support began to flood in on Tuesday.

"I am so deeply sorry. Rest in peace, Jesus," former Barcelona defender Gerard Pique, Andorra's majority shareholder, wrote on social media.

The RFEF postponed Tuesday's matches in the Copa del Rey and Copa de la Reina, "as a gesture of respect and solidarity."

#119. Denmark pushes for EU ban on ‘turbo chickens’ as secret footage causes outrage

Minister says government will work towards banning fast-growing breed across country and throughout EU

The Danish government is pursuing a national ban on “turbo chickens” and is calling for the fast-growing breed to be prohibited across Europe.

The move comes amid widespread outrage over allegations of lawbreaking by one of Denmark’s big chicken producers.

According to a TV2 documentary broadcast last week, producer Christian Tvergaard broke Danish law by giving chickens only half the amount of darkness a day that is required, in order to encourage them to eat more. The documentary featured secretly filmed footage from a chicken coop in North Jutland.

The Ross 308 breed, labelled the turbo chicken, is the most widespread in Danish poultry production. It grows from about 50 grams to 2kg in just 33 days. Such rapid growth can lead to problems with the animal’s bones and organs. Other chicken breeds take between 46 and 63 days to reach the same weight.

Denmark’s minister for nature and animal welfare, Christian Rabjerg Madsen, said he discouraged Danes from buying turbo chickens, telling the broadcaster: “I want to work for a national ban. But it’s clear that this is something that needs to be investigated both legally and economically.” He added: “Now we will work for a national ban, we will work for a ban at EU level.”

In response to the documentary, Tvergaard has admitted to making some wrong decisions. He told TV2: “I look inward and must admit that I have made some wrong decisions. There is no excuse for that, but I can learn from it and correct where I have not done well enough.

“And I have done that, both when it comes to the lighting programme and a number of the workflows we have in the stable, where both procedures and the roster have been tightened up.”

The proposal for the ban on turbo chickens is gaining political momentum and attracting cross-party support in Denmark. But industry figures warn that to be effective, any ban must include imported chickens as well.

Klement Hinkbøl Ribergaard, the chief executive of chicken producer Danpo, said: “If Denmark alone bans the production of fast-growing chickens while shops, restaurants and consumers in the Danish market can continue to buy imported, fast-growing chicken of more or less questionable quality, we risk a situation in which animal welfare certainly does not benefit.”

With fast-growing breeds so prominent across the EU, it would mark a landmark shift. According to Eurogroup for Animals, 95% of chickens in the bloc are now raised in intensive systems and bred to produce the cheapest amount of meat possible, creating a “race to the bottom”.

“Fast-growing breeds are inherently predisposed to suffering, with their bodies growing so quickly they can barely stand on their legs,” said Reineke Hameleers, the advocacy group’s chief executive. “Denmark’s political willingness to stop this is an example that should be followed by the EU, for it presents the progressiveness this industry needs, one that is backed up by scientific evidence.”

Britta Riis, chief executive of Animal Protection Denmark, said turbo chickens were the result of a “deeply unethical and grotesque form of breeding” that led to “systematic abuse” and considerable suffering for the birds. The organisation is calling for all chickens to be bred for the same amount of time as organic breeds, which is 56 days.

Animal welfare has featured heavily in public debate in recent months in Denmark. The issue of pig farming dominated the last general election to such an extent that it was nicknamed the “pig election”.

The country is due to stop mink farming by August 2029 after the current government decided not to seek an EU exemption on the practice.

#116. UK and EU agree deal to remove Brexit checks on food and animal products

Agreement to ease rules on food imports and exports is expected to come into force in first quarter of 2028

The EU and the UK have completed their deal to remove Brexit checks on imports and exports of food and animal products ranging from sausages and cheese to leather goods and flowers.

A wider series of mini-deals expected to be sealed at a UK-EU summit on 20 November would also result in Britain once again contributing to the EU budget as the price of rejoining a single electricity market.

It will prove a boon for British food exporters and will also mean smaller retailers in the UK such as delicatessens can return to buying smaller batches of cheese and wines, something that became impossible because of the cost of Brexit checks introduced in 2022.

But sources say the sanitary and phytosanitary (SPS) deal, largely agreed in May, will not be implemented until the first quarter of 2028 after the change in UK prime minister caused a six-month delay to the second EU-UK summit, now due to be held on 20 November.

Last month the National Farmers’ Union said exports of British food to the EU had collapsed by a third since Brexit, with eggs, chicken and potato products worst hit.

Sources said a 200-page text on post-Brexit food exports had “been settled and there will no further negotiation”.

A draft of a joint statement to be issued by the British prime minister, Andy Burnham, and EU leaders on 20 November is to be discussed by EU ambassadors at a meeting in Brussels on Wednesday.

It states the SPS deal would allow “the vast majority of movements of animals, animal products, plants and plant products between Great Britain and the crown dependencies and the EU to take place without the certificates or controls that are currently required”.

It adds that the UK goods would also be exempt from a carbon pollution tax, known as the carbon border adjustment mechanism, as the bloc and Britain are already aligned on emmissions.

Rejoining the EU’s single electricity market is designed to help stabilise supplies but will allow UK energy companies to sell into continental grids again.

The draft joint text says: “We agree that the UK’s participation in parts of the EU’s internal market requires an appropriate level of financial contribution towards reducing economic and social disparities between the regions of the EU that reflects the degree of the UK’s participation in the EU’s internal market”.

The deal has divided opinion among unionists in Northern Ireland, with Gavin Robinson, the leader of the Democratic Unionist party, saying it does not go far enough and reflects what he sees as a “lack of ambition” by the government.

The DUP, which is in turmoil after the MP Carla Lockhart’s decision to quit the party on Monday, supported Brexit but not the Windsor framework part of the withdrawal agreement because it cemented SPS checks on goods sold into Northern Ireland from Great Britain.

Steve Aiken, the Windsor framework spokesperson for the rival Ulster Unionist party, gave a cautious welcome to the deal. “If the agreement goes ahead, checks on most agricultural goods coming from the rest of our nation will be significantly, but not completely, reduced,” he said.

The SPS deal will be presented to EU ambassadors at a meeting on Wednesday that will discuss the first iteration of a joint statement to be issued by Andy Burnham and EU leaders on 20 November.

The “zero text” of a joint statement, as it is known, also includes chapters on a widely expected youth experience scheme, but this is still being negotiated, and ambassadors are also due to discuss an 11th-hour proposal from the UK over tuition fees for young Europeans to break a deadlock in talks.

Two weeks ago the European Commission was so concerned that the UK would not budge on its insistence that EU students should pay full international fees that the ambassadors discussed “decoupling” the issue and agreeing instead to negotiate on it separately for another six months.

The SPS deal was largely agreed in May and published by the government to allow food producers time to prepare for the change. At the time, the Department for Environment, Food and Rural Affairs said it expected the deal to be implemented in May next year.

Defra has now briefed industry leaders that it may not be implemented until 2028, with a further six-month transition period for a handful of key products such as grains grown this year with fungicides not yet approved in the EU.

The delay is caused by the UK’s need to “dynamically align” British food standards legislation with the EU’s. Although the UK has largely followed EU rules, it has diversified in some areas such as fungicides used to help crops grow in British wet weather.

The legislation is expected to renew controversy over whether the UK is a “rule taker” rather than a “rule maker”, a key issue in Brexit.

Under the proposed framework, retailers in Northern Ireland will no longer have to label products as “not for the EU”, a process criticised in the past by big supermarket chains such as Marks & Spencer.

Talks between British and EU officials began in London earlier this year with the aim of removing much of the Brexit paperwork for both sides. One transport leader told the parliamentary trade select committee that Brexit paperwork had been “pure hell”, with one lorry held up for as long as 27 days in Calais simply because it did not have the right certificates for a frozen beef cargo.

→ Baca artikel asli

#115. Can US midterm election really change Trump's Iran policy?

The US midterms are at hand, but their impact on the Iran war remains far from clear. Analysts say the Washington political elite may not be as divided on Iran as it appears.

The US election this Tuesday is shaping up to be one of the most consequential midterm votes in modern US history, with Washington's relationship with Israel and its war on Iran right at the center of the political standoff.

Many Democrats, including the party's rising stars such as Ohio Senate nominee Abdul El-Sayed, are outspoken critics of the Iran war, with El-Sayed calling the conflict "illegal" and "stupid."

US President Donald Trump sought to downplay the effects of the war during the campaign. Last week, he claimed only "one or two targets" remained in Iran and that those could be settled in 10 minutes. However, he immediately added that messages are still being exchanged, Tehran shows strong interest in a deal, and the US has plenty of time to deal with the crisis.

At the same time, there are reports of the US and Israel preparing to launch a new round of strikes on Iran just after the midterm election and pro-war Republicans such as Senator Ted Cruz are putting pressure on the White House to deal with "the threat" posed by Iran.

"I have urged arming the Iranian protesters to overthrow the regime," Cruz said in an online post after spending a day with Trump.

Behind the scenes, analysts warn that the Washington political elite may not be as divided on Iran as it appears. Moreover, even a resounding Democrat victory might not give them enough leverage on the Trump administration regarding the Iran war.

Talking to DW, former National Security Advisor John Bolton said the Democrats are likely to secure a majority in the House of Representatives and possibly the Senate, handing them control of the Iran war funding.

The US Congress "can allocate defense budgets or impose restrictions on the use of financial resources," Bolton said.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Nevertheless, Bolton points out that the US president can veto congressional legislation, which would then require a two-thirds majority in both assemblies to overcome.

"Even if Democrats hold a majority in both houses, their influence will be limited to the legislative and political tools of Congress," he said.

US diplomat and Iran expert Alan Eyre points to a kind of "bipartisan consensus" that Republicans and Democrats have on Iran.

"Both major US parties share significant common ground in their opposition to the political structure of the Islamic Republic and in their support for policies aimed at exerting pressure on Tehran to change its behavior," he said.

While warning of a "crushing response" to any new strikes on underground sites, Tehran has confirmed ongoing indirect communication via Doha and Muscat, keeping its diplomatic option open.

Shahin Modarres, an international security analyst leading the Iran team at ITSS Verona, told  DW that Tehran's actions are based on a miscalculation. The regime, he argues, assumes that a Democratic Congress can easily restrain the White House.

Tehran's strategy in dragging out peace talks, pressuring the Strait of Hormuz, and letting fuel prices weigh on US voters, aims to deny Trump a win before November. However, Modarres notes this strategy may backfire after the election, as the Trump administration will no longer have any reason to delay its moves.

This approach also ignores the fact that both US parties largely agree on not letting Iranian develop nuclear weapons, he said.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Trump's ally-turned-critic John Bolton believes that Trump will only decide on his next steps on Iran after the election, once the political pressure grows less acute.

"I cannot predict what the US President will do; even Trump himself does not know his final decision right now," he told DW.

Bolton cites as an example a possible US attack on "Kuh-e Kolang," the new nuclear site he claims the Iranian government is constructing near Natanz.

"In this scenario, Trump could damage these facilities, declare victory, and pull back."

Trump has consistently emphasized that the US goal is to deny Iran the capability of acquiring nuclear weapons. Throughout the conflict, the US president has also repeatedly claimed his administration had succeeded in this regard.

Bolton, in contrast, believes that the Islamic Republic will continue to pursue nuclear weapons capability for as long as it remains in power.

The former Trump advisor views "regime change" as the only sustainable solution to keep Iran from acquiring nuclear weapons.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Eyre, the first Persian-speaking diplomat to be the spokesperson for the US Department of State, emphasizes the difficulty of using external tools to bring about sustainable change. In his view, even if there is a consensus in Washington opposing the Islamic Republic and demanding a change in its behavior, this US consensus alone cannot overcome the structural obstacles to political change inside Iran. He reiterates that any fundamental transformation must emerge from within Iranian society itself.

The veteran diplomat also points to the failure of "Washington's public diplomacy to convey a clear, coherent, and authentic message to Iranian society."

He notes that official US government messaging is only incompletely reflected in the media outlets that the vast majority of Iranian citizens rely on for news.

"Furthermore, targeted and principled engagement with the core of Iranian society has generally not been a priority for successive US administrations," he said.

"Iranian citizens see that the US takes certain positions at the rhetorical level, but behaves differently in practice."

According to Eyre, this gap between words and deeds in Washington is a structural phenomenon that is not limited to a single administration or a single election cycle.

#110. Russia bombs humanitarian hub in Ukraine, killing at least 31 people

Other overnight strikes targeted power plants across Ukraine, damaged hospitals and wounded scores of people

Russia has bombed a humanitarian hub in southern Ukraine, killing at least 31 people and leaving dozens missing, as part of a wave of strikes across the country.

Other overnight Russian strikes targeted power plants across Ukraine, damaged hospitals and wounded scores of people, as the Ukrainian president, Volodymyr Zelenskyy, warned that the US was distracted by its midterm elections.

In total, Russia had launched 289 attack drones, 17 ballistic missiles and 59 cruise missiles across nine regions, triggering emergency power cuts, Zelenskyy said in an online post.

“The Russians think America won’t notice this signal of theirs,” he said. “But we need to notice and stop such terror. We need to help Ukraine defend its skies; otherwise, Russia will never stop its missile strikes,” he said.

The barrage came just a day after a rare local ceasefire in neighbouring Kherson ended, underscoring the limits of the temporary truce.

The deadliest guided-bomb attack first targeted a school building used to distribute humanitarian aid in Komyshuvakha, a village in Ukraine’s southern Zaporizhzhia region, and then took aim at a nearby shelter.

The dead included four police officers and two rescue workers, Mykhailo Semikin, the acting head of the regional military administration, said on Tuesday. At least five people were hurt. Semikin said 48 people were considered missing.

As night fell in Komyshuvakha, emergency responders used torches to search through the rubble of collapsed buildings, according to photos and video posted online by local officials.

Efforts to rescue people on Monday evening were hindered as Russian drones launched additional attacks in the area that had been bombed, according to Mykhailo Semikin, a military official in Zaporizhzhia.

Zelenskyy said the Russians could not have been unaware that residents remaining in Komyshuvakha gathered each week to pick up humanitarian packages from the site.

The attack, he said, had shown that Moscow was intent on killing people instead of building on the past week’s ceasefire in the embattled southern city of Oleshky, brokered by the US first lady, Melania Trump.

“Russians continue to demonstrate, time and again, their hatred of Ukraine and everything that exists here,” Zelenskyy said.

In the north-east region of Kharkiv, a combined Russian missile and drone attack had wounded at least 62 people, local authorities said. A strike damaged a medical facility in the Nemyshlianskyi district, shattered windows in four apartment buildings and damaged cars. More than 200 patients had been transferred to other medical facilities, said the head of the regional military administration, Oleh Syniehubov.

“If Russia genuinely ‌wanted de-escalation, there was an opportunity following the situation in Oleshky to continue taking humanitarian steps. We proposed this. But Russia continues to kill people,” Zelenskyy said.

The ceasefire last week enabled an evacuation of the Russian-occupied Oleshky. It was the longest pause in fighting between the neighbours in the war, now in its fifth year, which has killed hundreds of thousands of people and displaced millions.

The Kremlin said that no other similar missions were being planned “at the moment”.

US-led talks between Russia and Ukraine were due to resume next week, the US special envoy Steve Witkoff said on Monday.

Donald Trump, who pledged during his election campaign to end the war in a single day, has repeatedly pushed for a settlement but has so far failed to secure a breakthrough.

The latest round of talks is scheduled for 10-11 November in Abu Dhabi, according to Witkoff, amid growing concerns in Kyiv and among its European allies over Trump’s increasingly close ties with Moscow and discussions of potential business deals between the US and Russia.

Russia announced on Tuesday that its delegation would be led by Vladimir Medinsky, a hardline former culture minister who has headed several previous rounds of negotiations with Ukraine.

Medinsky’s earlier appointment as chief negotiator was met with scepticism by Ukrainian officials and analysts, who questioned whether he had the authority to make meaningful concessions. A key architect of the historical revisionism used by Putin to justify his invasion of Ukraine, Medinsky has long promoted the Kremlin’s narrative that Ukraine is inseparable from Russia.

Zelenskyy, meanwhile, arrived in Berlin for talks with the German chancellor, Friedrich Merz. Germany has become Ukraine’s largest provider of military aid since Merz took office in May 2025, overtaking the US as American support has declined.

The Ukrainian president is also expected to attend this year’s Berlin Security Conference.

Reuters and Agence France-Presse contributed to this report

#108. Beijing promised to 'fight back' over Taiwan leader's US visit. But this time it has more to lose

An anticipated meeting between Taiwan’s President Tsai Ing-wen and US House Speaker Kevin McCarthy in California this week has sparked concerns of a repeat of the pressure campaign China launched last year when then-Speaker Nancy Pelosi visited Taipei.

At that time, Beijing encircled the island democracy with unprecedented military drills – firing multiple missiles into its surrounding waters and sending dozens of warplanes speeding across a sensitive median line dividing the Taiwan Strait.

It also cut off contact with the United States over a number of issues from military matters to combating climate change, in retaliation for what it viewed as a violation of its sovereignty.

This time, Beijing has already threatened to “resolutely fight back” if a Tsai-McCarthy meeting goes ahead.

It also slammed Washington for allowing Tsai to stopover in the US while en route to and from official visits in Central America, warning it could lead to “serious” confrontation between the two powers.

A defiant Tsai staked out her own ground, pledging as she took off on her 10-day trip not to let “external pressure” stop Taiwan from connecting with the world and like-minded democracies.

But the optics of the meeting, taking place in California and not Taiwan, and its timing – at a particularly thorny moment in China’s foreign relations and ahead of a presidential election in Taiwan that could reset the tone of its relationship with Beijing – may see Beijing tread more carefully this time, or at least not escalate further, analysts say.

“This puts the burden on China not to overreact, because any overreaction is only going to push China further away from the world,” said Yun Sun, director of the China Program at the Stimson Center think tank in Washington.

That doesn’t mean, however, that Beijing won’t be closely watching Tsai’s movements as it calibrates its response – and decides how much military might to flex over her meeting with an American lawmaker on American soil.

The opacity of China’s system – and the potential for competing interests within its vast bureaucracy – also make it difficult to accurately predict its response.

“Every time Taiwan does anything that China doesn’t like, the Chinese react with their own military coercion,” Sun said. But in the current situation, “they have to consider the consequences of overreaction,” she added.

The expected meeting, which McCarthy’s office announced earlier this week would take place on Wednesday, also comes at a precarious moment in US-China relations.

Washington and Beijing are struggling to stabilize their communication amid flaring tensions over issues from a downed suspected Chinese surveillance balloon to semiconductor supply chains – raising the stakes of potential damage to that relationship if Beijing lashes out as it did when Tsai met Pelosi.

Taiwan is still feeling the fallout of that response last August, with Chinese military forces now regularly making incursions over what had previously been an informal but largely respected border of control between Beijing and Taipei in the Taiwan Strait. Taiwan’s official Central News Agency also reported Monday that Tsai would meet with McCarthy, citing Tsai’s presidential office.

But a meeting between Tsai and the leader of the Republican majority in the US House of Representatives, who ranks second in line to the Presidency, would mark another symbolic moment for Taiwan and the US, which only maintain unofficial ties.

For Tsai, who is entering the final year of her two-term presidency, “it’s clearly a capstone event,” according to Wen-Ti Sung, a political scientist at the Australian National University’s Taiwan Studies Program. “She has this image as the Taiwanese president who has taken US-Taiwan relations to new heights, and who … has been able to give Taiwan almost unprecedented international visibility,” he said.

That increased visibility – and enhanced cooperation with the US – has followed China’s mounting pressure on the island, which sits fewer than 110 miles (177 kilometers) from the mainland coast.

China’s Communist Party claims the self-governing island democracy as its own despite never having controlled it, and has vowed to take the island, by force if necessary.

China thinks it's diplomatically isolating Taiwan. It isn't

The party has undertaken a sweeping expansion of its military capabilities over the past decade under leader Xi Jinping – and ramped up its pervasive economic, diplomatic and military pressure on Taiwan.

That’s driven concerns, among some in Washington, that Beijing is preparing for an invasion, though China’s official language still suggests that scenario is not its preferred option for achieving the claimed goal of “reunification.”

It is those pressures – and how to support Taiwan against unilateral actions by Beijing – that are likely to be on the table when Tsai, McCarthy and a bipartisan group of US lawmakers sit down on Wednesday.

Congress has been a pillar of increasing American support for Taiwan in recent years. Lawmakers regularly visit the island and drive bipartisan legislation enhancing support and cooperation.

While the US switched its diplomatic relations to Beijing decades ago, it maintains unofficial ties with Taiwan and is bound by law to provide the democratic island with the means to defend itself.

Under Washington’s longstanding “One China” policy, the US acknowledges China’s position that Taiwan is part of China, but has never officially recognized Beijing’s claim to the island of 23 million.

Though McCarthy does not have Pelosi’s decades-long record of advocacy regarding China, the California Republican is now a leading voice pushing for closer scrutiny of Beijing, and meeting Tsai could help him to burnish that image.

Last month, McCarthy told reporters that meeting Tsai in the US would not impact whether he travels to Taiwan in the future – something he had earlier said he wanted to do.

A meeting in California, on US soil, is widely seen as less likely to provoke Beijing than a McCarthy visit to Taiwan.

Pelosi’s trip – the first from a lawmaker of that rank to the island in 25 years – generated a fever pitch of nationalist and anti-US rhetoric in mainland China.

This time, so far, domestic conversation in China’s heavily controlled media sphere has been significantly muted.

But the stakes remain high – including for Beijing itself – over how it responds, analysts say.

As Taiwan prepares for a presidential election in January, a fierce response could push voters away from Taiwan’s main opposition party Kuomintang (KMT), widely seen as more friendly toward Beijing.

It could also jar with another high profile trip happening now: a tour of mainland China from former Taiwan president and senior KMT member Ma Ying-jeou, the first visit from a current or former Taiwan leader since the end of the Chinese Civil War in 1949.

Ma’s tour is a “once in a half a century opportunity to send a conciliatory message between the two sides, Beijing shouldn’t want to tank that,” said Sung, the political scientist.

What you need to know about China-Taiwan tensions

China is also acutely aware that its actions toward Taiwan are under a significantly brighter global spotlight following the invasion of Ukraine by Russian President Vladimir Putin, a close diplomatic partner of Xi. Putin’s rhetoric over Ukraine has echoes of how Xi speaks of Taiwan.

Beijing has recently sought to position itself as an agent of peace in that conflict – especially as it aims to repair frayed ties with Europe.

This week, as Tsai is expected to meet with McCarthy, French President Emmanuel Macron and European Commission President Ursula von der Leyen will head to China – an important opportunity that Xi may not want to overshadow with military posturing.

An aggressive response also risks stoking confrontation with the US, not yet six months after Xi and US President Joe Biden called to enhance communication during a face-to-face meeting in Bali.

“(A less overtly aggressive response) would imply that Beijing does not wish to escalate tension with the US to a level that can risk getting out of hand,” said Steve Tsang, director of the SOAS China Institute in London.

“A reset in US-China ties is not on the agenda, but an easing of tension is not beyond the realm of possibility.”

If you are joining us now, we are following reports about at least four planes affected by bomb threats across Europe this morning: two in Poland (from the UK 10:33, 10:58, 12:00 and Denmark 11:33, 12:59), one in Bulgaria (from Israel 12:50), and one in Spain (from the UK 12:44).

Over in Sweden, centrist leader Elisabeth Thand Ringqvist has just lost a vote on her proposed government – which is hardly surprising given that even she as very clear she had no chance of winning.

It’s been 51 days since the Swedish parliamentary election, and it’s the second failed attempt to form the government. Two more and we get a snap election. Gulp.

The next attempt is expected to be led by the interim centre-right prime minister, Ulf Kristersson, but his outgoing coalition is also unlikely to secure enough support in the parliament, further complicating the process.

#103. Australia’s social media ban ‘ill-thought-out’, Reddit argues in high court challenge

Reddit challenges ban on grounds it impinges implied freedom of political communications for teens

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

The Australian government’s rush to pass the under-16s social media ban legislation in 2024 led to “ill-thought-out, over-broad legislation” that has since been narrowed after Reddit sued the government, lawyers for the social media company told the high court on Tuesday.

Reddit, one of the top 10 websites visited in the world, has challenged the teen social media ban on the grounds it impinges the implied freedom of political communication for under-16s.

In oral arguments before the high court on Tuesday, Reddit’s counsel, Perry Herzfeld SC, argued in the years leading up to the ban legislation, a 2021 parliamentary inquiry on social media had not recommended introducing a ban, with the eSafety commissioner warning the inquiry at the time it would be a “blunt force approach”.

When the government announced the ban in 2024, Reddit argued the legislation was introduced and passed within a week, with only 24 hours of submissions allowed.

“A process of this kind is likely to lead to ill-thought-out, over-broad legislation,” Herzfeld argued.

After eSafety announced Reddit would be included in the ban, and Reddit did not accept it met the definition of a social media platform under the law, the company announced the high court challenge to the law in late 2025.

In March this year, the communications minister, Anika Wells, introduced new rules as part of the legislation that defines additional requirements for social media companies to meet the definition including logged-in features, recommendation features or endless scroll.

Herzfeld argued the ban does not address those factors specifically by a broad ban on under-16s accounts, and has argued it would be able to under different legislation – pointing to the digital duty of care legislation currently before the parliament.

Outside the court, Wells told reporters she wasn’t the minister at the time the legislation passed, but said the government’s arguments in response would be made in court.

Since the ban came into effect in December last year, more than 5 million user accounts the platforms understand belong to people aged under 16 across 10 platforms have been suspended or deactivated.

The platforms currently covered by the ban include Snapchat, TikTok, YouTube, Facebook, Instagram, Reddit, Kick, X, Twitch and Threads.

The case centres around whether the effect of the ban limiting political communications is justified by what the government has said is a “credible risk” of harms such as mental health issues, sleeping problems and eating disorder issues.

Herzfeld told the court on Tuesday the ban prevents teenagers from actively engaging in political communications through subreddits such as r/Australia. While they can still view the content without an account, they can’t make their own posts, comment or upvote content, Reddit argued.

Justice Jacqueline Gleeson expressed reservations when pointed to examples of high-profile teens being politically engaged in social media – such as 6News’ Leo Puglisi, who began his news site at the age of 11.

Gleeson said the “vast majority of teens the vast majority of time” do not engage in politics while under 16.

When asked whether teens were being limited in engaging in political communication on just those platforms covered by the ban, Herzfeld argued the platforms offer teens a much wider audience compared with “shouting on the street” or being interviewed in the media.

Herzfeld said the Australian government had also cited a number of studies in its submissions outlining the “credible risks” of harm that the ban is aiming to address, but none of the studies cited had mentioned Reddit. He conceded Reddit was not arguing the parliament had to wait for scientific consensus on the link between the harmful design features of social media and the harms.

At the end of its argument on Tuesday, Reddit outlined a number of alternatives the government could have pursued, including the digital duty of care bill currently before the parliament, as well as laws prohibiting push notifications, or limiting the time children can spend on the platform, or requiring child accounts to only be allowed with parental control.

Government barrister Joanna Davidson SC, told the court the design features outlined in the rules, which she said are designed to maximise engagement on the platforms, are “broadly shared” across all 10 platforms, including Reddit.

Those features include autoplay video, recommendation systems, notifications by default and surfacing the most popular content.

Davidson said the platforms encourage users to stay to maximise user engagement and bring in more revenue. Those features can have a “compounding” risk of harm when working together.

When people keep returning to social media for fear of missing out, it can lead to issues such as a lack of sleep, or an increased likelihood of depression, she said.

Children are more vulnerable to the risks due to their cognitive stage of development, Davidson said.

The government will continue to make its argument on Wednesday.

The Digital Freedom Project, led by New South Wales Libertarian MP John Ruddick, adopted Reddit’s arguments against the law, and only sought to be heard on the issue of whether as a group advocating for its members it had standing to bring the case.

The court heard the group had “at least one” member aged between 13 and 15 who was affected by the ban.

Ruddick was joined outside the court this morning by the original plaintiff for the case – 16-year-old Noah Jones – and his mother, Renee, as well as One Nation senator Malcolm Roberts. Jones and fellow teen Macey Nyland withdrew as plaintiffs in the case due to the legal cost risk if the case went against them.
