#102. Suspected Chinese spy balloon was able to transmit information back to Beijing

The Chinese spy balloon that transited the US earlier this year was able to capture imagery and collect some signals intelligence from US military sites, a source familiar with the matter tells CNN.

The balloon was able to transmit information back to Beijing in real time, the source said, and the US government still does not know for sure whether the Chinese government could wipe the balloon’s data as it received it. That raises questions about whether there is intelligence the balloon was able to gather that the US still doesn’t know about.

Still, the intelligence community has not been overly concerned about the information the balloon was able to gather, the person said, because it is not much more sophisticated than what Chinese satellites are able to glean as they orbit over similar locations.

A US intelligence official told CNN on Monday that “although analysis of the wreckage of the High Altitude Balloon remains ongoing, thus far, its flight over the United States does not appear to have provided critical new insights to the People’s Republic of China.”

The US also knew what the balloon’s path would be and was able to protect sensitive sites and censor some signals before the balloon was able to pick them up, officials have said.

As CNN has reported, the US intelligence community last year developed a method of tracking what it says is a fleet of these Chinese balloons operating across the globe, controlled by the Chinese military.

The FBI is still examining the balloon, but so far officials have been able to glean additional information about how the device worked, including the algorithms used for the balloon’s software and how it is powered and designed.

CNN has reached out to the National Security Council at the White House and the Pentagon for comment. NBC was first to report on the new intelligence.

The balloon first crossed into US airspace over Alaska in late January before passing through Canada and down into Montana, where it hovered for a few days, leading the US to believe it was trying to surveil sensitive military sites, like Malmstrom Air Force Base in Montana. It was eventually shot down by the US off the East Coast on February 4, and the incident further escalated tensions between Washington and Beijing, including the postponement of a diplomatic visit by US Secretary of State Antony Blinken to China.

A senior State Department official said in February that, as the balloon floated across the US, it “was capable of conducting signals intelligence collection operations.”

Gen. Glen VanHerck, the commander of US Northern Command and NORAD, said at the time that the US “did not assess” that the balloon “presented a significant collection hazard beyond what already exists in actionable technical means from the Chinese.”

The surveillance program, which includes a number of similar balloons, is in part run out of the small Chinese province of Hainan, officials told CNN. The US does not know the precise size of the fleet of Chinese surveillance balloons, but sources tell CNN that the program has conducted at least two dozen missions over at least five continents in recent years.

Roughly half a dozen of those flights have been within US airspace, although not necessarily over US territory, according to one official familiar with the intelligence.

China has maintained that the balloon was actually just a weather balloon thrown off course, and the US has been assessing the possibility that it was not deliberately maneuvered into the continental US by the Chinese government, CNN has reported.

Still, China did maintain some ability to maneuver the balloon, officials believe. And once the balloon was over Montana, China appeared to take advantage of its position to loiter over sensitive sites and try to collect intelligence.

This story has been updated with additional reporting.

CNN’s Alex Marquardt contributed to this report.

#100. RBA interest rates: Bullock says ‘pain now’ will prevent worse later as Reserve Bank hikes rates for fifth time

Reserve Bank of Australia increases official cash rate to 4.85%, bringing it to highest level since 2008

RBA interest rates live updates: Reserve Bank cash rate decision

Get our daily political email, free app or daily news podcast

The Reserve Bank governor Michele Bullock has said that “some pain now will prevent even worse pain down the track”, after lifting official interest rates to an 18-year high of 4.85% in a bid to combat persistent inflation.

The RBA’s decision to hit mortgage holders for a fifth time in 2026 will intensify pressure on indebted households, but there were signs that the central bank will at least now wait through summer to gauge the impact of higher interest rates on the economy.

The decision split the monetary policy board, as six members voted for a hike, while three were in favour of a steady cash rate. Financial markets halved the probability of another rate rise before Christmas to 12%, but continued to price in a roughly 30% chance of a sixth hike by May.

Sign up for Guardian Australia’s Politics, really newsletter here

Speaking to journalists shortly after the rate decision, Bullock said: “I know this is not the news that many Australians want to hear, but it is what’s needed to get inflation down.”

“Inflation is too high, and our forecast suggests it will stay higher for longer. Some pain now will prevent even worse pain down the track,” she said.

The back-to-back hikes will heap more pain on over 3.3m households with home loans and turn the screws on a federal government under pressure for its economic management.

The latest rate rise will lift the monthly interest payment on a $700,000 home loan by $107, according to Canstar, bringing the total rise in repayments over this year’s five hikes to $532.

Jim Chalmers, the treasurer, said higher fuel prices due to the Middle East conflict were the “primary driver” for the recent inflationary surge.

“Our budgets are not what’s putting upward pressure on inflation. But we can play a helpful role and that’s what we intend to do,” Chalmers said.

With surveys showing Australians are increasingly inclined to blame the Albanese government for ongoing high cost of living, Angus Taylor, the opposition leader, said voters “deserved to hear the truth”.

“Labor’s spending spree is driving up inflation, it’s driving up interest rates, and Australians are paying the price,” Taylor said.

The RBA’s post-meeting statement removed an explicit reference to the need for further hikes – an omission that economists at UBS interpreted as “clearly dovish”.

And with the cash rate now at its highest level since 2008, Cherelle Murphy, the chief economist at EY, said the RBA “is likely to take a wait-and-see approach over summer”, even as she said higher interest rates could not be ruled out.

The RBA’s latest set of forecasts in its Statement on Monetary Policy (SOMP) showed a difficult period ahead for the economy.

The forecasts suggested the country’s cost of living crisis would extend for another year, even as Australia struggles with below trend economic growth and climbing unemployment.

Inflation will push higher through the final months of 2026 to about 4.5% and only return below 3% – within the bank’s 2% to 3% target range – by the end of next year, or nine months later than hoped.

Global oil prices would ease over the coming 18 months, but still remain relatively elevated, according to the forecasts, suggesting limited relief from high fuel costs for motorists and ongoing high costs for businesses.

“We view the risks to the inflation outlook as still skewed to the upside,” the report said.

Alongside persistent cost of living pressures, the RBA also predicted weak economic growth through to the end of 2028.

The economy would grow by 1.6% this year and by 1.3% in 2027.

That would be well below an estimated potential growth rate of about 2%, but better than recent forecasts by bank analysts.

And despite the risks associated with a housing downturn that “is one of the steepest over the past 50 years”, the central banks’ economists saw no evidence that the country was headed towards recession over the coming two years.

Unemployment, now at 4.7%, would end next year at about 4.9%, before creeping over 5% in 2028.

#97. Major Queensland hospital to close pathology labs amid potential formaldehyde exposure investigation

About 400 staff affected by closure at both Mater’s Brisbane and Springfield hospitals, with financial sustainability of pathology business a factor

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

In-house pathology services at one of Queensland’s biggest hospitals will permanently close amid an investigation into potential formaldehyde exposure and continual financial strain.

The closure, which affects 400 staff across Mater’s Brisbane and Springfield hospitals, includes the lab which handles foetus and newborn tissue and cell testing for unexpected deaths.

This Brisbane facility was closed with short notice on 24 September due safety concerns over potential formalin exposure.

Formalin is a liquid comprising water and formaldehyde and is considered a hazardous chemical, making it subject to strict workplace safety protocols. Exposure can cause eye, nose and respiratory irritation, trigger allergies, and is associated with increased risk of some cancers.

Mandatory ventilation standards and safe specimen handling protocols are used to manage exposure among laboratory staff.

Anatomical pathology laboratory staff at the Mater Brisbane were also required to wear protective respiratory equipment as a precaution. Wearing respiratory gear is not standard protocol for laboratories except in the cases of a formalin spill, or where there is concern levels might be above exposure standards.

Staff also wore personal exposure monitoring devices and, after 17 samples were taken on 21 September, an employee returned a result above workplace exposure standards, prompting the lab to be closed on 24 September with staff not told when it might reopen.

A Mater spokesperson said an investigation is ongoing into the abnormal result.

On 2 November, after questions from Guardian Australia about the closure, a Mater spokesperson said all pathology services at Mater’s Brisbane and Springfield hospitals will be outsourced through specialist providers, including Sullivan Nicolaides Pathology and Pathology Queensland.

This includes not only the anatomical pathology laboratory, but also all other pathology services at both hospitals, the spokesperson said.

An internal email sent to staff across the pathology departments on Tuesday, seen by Guardian Australia, said: “At the end of last month, we identified infrastructure and safety concerns within our anatomical pathology service and made the immediate decision to suspend that service.

“It has become clear there is no quick or simple solution.”

The spokesperson said that, while a number of controls were introduced to mitigate safety risks, significant long-term infrastructure changes and investment would be required to reopen the lab.

This work “cannot be considered in isolation from the broader sustainability of Mater’s pathology business”, the spokesperson said, adding that it was the overall review of all challenges facing pathology services that prompted the permanent closure and outsourcing.

“The pathology sector has changed significantly in recent years and is increasingly reliant on scale, specialist workforces, technology, infrastructure and logistics,” the spokesperson said.

“Mater’s current operating model is not sustainable over the longer term.”

The decision will have no impact on the high-quality patient care, the spokesperson said, and will allow more resources to be directed to clinical care of patients.

Mater Brisbane is a not-for-profit major tertiary hospital jointly funded by Queensland Health and Mater Private Hospitals. The Springfield hospital is a public and private facility.

The internal email said staff had “lived with questions about the future of the service for some time”.

A Queensland Health spokesperson said Mater Health is responsible for decisions regarding its pathology services.

“While most services have transitioned to private pathology providers, Pathology Queensland has taken on a small increase in perinatal autopsies, which is being managed within existing capacity,” the spokesperson said.

“Queensland Health will continue to work with Mater to ensure patients have access to the pathology services they need.”

United Workers Union Queensland secretary, Fiona Scalon, said staff will be significantly affected if redeployment opportunities are not available, and said different employers operate under different enterprise agreements and conditions.

“This is not simply a change of employer,” she said. “It could mean workers losing a substantial part of their income and conditions, despite continuing to perform the same work.”

Do you know more? melissa.davey@theguardian.com

#95. Musk pumps $446m into US midterms, as campaign spending set to 'shatter' records

The US midterms campaign is set to be the most expensive on record - with the total cost projected to reach nearly $12bn (Â£9bn), according to Open Secrets, an independent organisation that tracks campaign finance.

Most of the spending is driven by outside political groups - known as Super Political Action Committees (Super Pacs) - pouring large sums of money into some key races ahead of election day on 3 November.

The top individual donor is billionaire businessman Elon Musk, who has spent almost half a billion dollars backing Republican candidates, particularly in Texas where many of his companies are based.

BBC Verify has looked into the figures and where the money was spent.

Follow live updates on US midterm elections

There are rules on how much people, parties and political action committees (Pacs) can give directly to candidates.

But Super Pacs can accept unlimited contributions from individuals, corporations, labour unions, and other political committees. They can't give that money directly to candidates but they can spend it supporting them indirectly.

According to the latest Federal Election Commission, external (FEC) filings which run to mid-October, more than $9.5bn had been spent in this midterms cycle.

Pacs and Super Pacs make up more than half of that - at about $5.5bn - with their spending in the last couple of months almost doubling.

Candidates, parties and outside groups have to report their spending to the FEC, but the full totals won't be available until after the midterm elections.

However, Open Secrets has projected the total will be $11.9bn based on trends in past cycles.

This would be about 30% more than what was spent during the last midterm elections in 2022.

The top 40 donors gave $2.4bn up until 14 October, according to the liberal-leaning think tank Brennan Center for Justice, external, which is a record high for that point in a midterms cycle.

"Spending in the 2026 midterms is shattering records, with more of the money coming from the wealthiest donors and groups this election," says Michael Beckel, a US campaign finance expert.

As well as individuals, organisations can also give to Pacs. For example, Geosor Corp and Fund for Policy Reform, both founded by the billionaire financier George Soros, have given a combined $102m to liberal groups.

Aside from Soros, former New York City mayor and businessman Michael Bloomberg has been the biggest Democratic donor, giving more than $60m.

Several billionaires including Jeff Yass, who runs one of the world's largest trading firms, and Richard and Elizabeth Uihlein, the owners of a US business supply company, have given over $100m this campaign to Republican causes, according to FEC filings.

But Musk has given by far the most of any individual, donating almost half a billion dollars as of October.

Musk has given most of this to a Super Pac he set up in 2024 called America PAC, as well as to the Republican-aligned Senate Leadership Fund.

Most of his money has been spent on the Texas Senate race, supporting Republican candidate Ken Paxton, who is running against Democratic candidate James Talarico.

Elon Musk worked for the Trump administration last year and has attended events at the White House since

Three takeaways from Elon Musk's latest political donations

America PAC's investments in the Texas race total more than $45m. It has also invested around $10m into both of the Senate races in Michigan and Ohio.

"Crypto companies have also been very active this cycle, with Ripple and Coinbase combining to give $63 million to fund Super Pacs promoting the crypto industry's interests," says Brendan J Glavin of Open Secrets.

Campaigns and political groups spend money trying to reach voters in a variety of ways - including hiring staff to do on-the-ground voter outreach and hosting campaign events like rallies. They also spend money to conduct internal polling.

However, the majority of funds are spent on advertising - especially on TV.

In this cycle, $11.15bn has been spent on advertising up until the end of October, according to ad tracking firm AdImpact, external.

"Traditional broadcast television remains the top beneficiary of advertising expenditures, though expenditures on digital ads continue to increase," says Beckel.

Similarly to Musk's spending, AdImpact tracked that most of the money has been spent on advertising in the Senate races in Michigan, Texas and Ohio, followed by Iowa and Maine.

#93. Australia seeks to change visa deal with UK after influx of Britons

Australia says easing of rules has led to surge in British visitor numbers, putting pressure on local workforce

The Australian prime minister, Anthony Albanese, has told Andy Burnham he is looking at changing rules for British backpackers in Australia, after an increase in the number of travellers visiting popular hotspots such as Sydney.

Albanese, who met Burnham on Monday evening at the Commonwealth heads of government meeting (Chogm) in the Caribbean, told reporters he wanted to discuss changing the agreement between the UK and Australia that removes a regional work requirement for British travellers.

Backpackers had previously been required to do about three months of regional work as part of visa conditions to help with the country’s work shortages in some occupations. But under the free trade agreement signed under Boris Johnson, Britons do not have to undertake the rural work that many other nationalities do as a condition of their visas. It also extended the maximum period for the visa to three years and the age limit to 35.

The Australian government has said the changes have led to traveller numbers more than doubling, from 40,000 two years ago to 87,000 last year, the majority concentrated in Sydney and beachside suburbs. The easing of visa restrictions also applies to Australians coming to the UK but fewer than 10,000 came last year.

Albanese told the Australian parliament that the numbers of Britons arriving to a small destination was putting pressure on the local workforce. “They are staying at Bondi Beach or Coogee or around particularly the capital cities and working in hospitality there. That’s placing enormous pressure because it’s distorting the labour market in these areas as well,” he said.

Burnham confirmed to reporters that he was meeting Albanese but declined to comment on any potential changes, saying “we’ll see” when asked if it would be on the agenda.

A Downing Street spokesperson said there had been discussions about the visas at a bilateral between the two leaders in Antigua at the summit but stopped short of making any commitments.

“The leaders discussed the increasingly uncertain global landscape and agreed that strong partnerships between close allies like the UK and Australia are more important than ever,” a readout of the meeting said.

“Turning to the close relationship between the UK and Australia, the leaders underlined the importance of ensuring opportunities for young people both at home and abroad. On broader migration, the prime minister updated on the new rules being rolled out for migrants looking to settle in the UK, and the leaders discussed how best to drive down illegal migration.

“They both looked forward to continuing their discussions during Chogm and staying in close contact.”

#92. Democrats have high hopes for midterms - but are the polls right?

The Democratic Party has high expectations as it heads into these pivotal US midterm elections.

A historically unpopular Republican president, an American public unhappy with the rising costs of food and bills, and months of favourable polls point to a good night for them on Tuesday.

Democratic candidates lead in well over the 218 seats needed to flip control of the lower chamber of Congress, the House of Representatives, the polls suggest. And they are tied or narrowly ahead in the key Senate races.

A recent New York Times poll had the Senate race in South Carolina, a state Donald Trump won by 18 percentage points in 2024, as a tie. Recent CNN surveys put the Democrats ahead in Texas, Kansas and Maine.

Recent US political history is littered with examples where public opinion polls, even ones just days before an election, were off the mark. Trump outperformed predictions on his way to White House victories in 2016 and 2024. His narrow defeat in 2020 came after most surveys indicated a more comfortable win for Democrat Joe Biden.

Earlier this year, polls suggested Democrat Abdul El-Sayed was poised for a double-digit win in Michigan's Democratic Senate primary. Instead it was 1%. In Wisconsin's Democratic primary for governor, the candidate that had a polling lead that approached 20 points lost by a fraction of a percent.

Democrats have an identity crisis - it won't be an easy fix

According to Clifford Young, president of Ipsos Public Affairs, which conducts political and market research surveys, polling has become difficult in recent years because it's harder to reach Americans as they abandon their land lines and screen their calls. And many of those who pollsters do contact have become less willing to participate.

"Conservatives, especially those on the far right, are much more anti-establishment by nature," Young says. "And they don't participate in anything. They hate the system. That's the challenge."

If Republican voters are being undercounted in a systematic way, and polling companies don't take that into consideration when constructing their voting models, it could skew their data towards the Democrats.

That's one of the reasons why it has become trickier for pollsters to predict what the electorate will look like.

IN DEPTH: The real reasons there are so few young US politicians

EXPLORE: Where the US midterms will be won and lost

VERIFY: How the US economy is really doing under Trump

NEWSLETTER: Sign up for Anthony Zurcher's analysis in your inbox

One explanation for why Trump overperformed in previous presidential elections is that his supporters â many of whom were not regular voters â flocked to the polls in higher-than-expected numbers.

That unpredictability is one of the main contributing factors to those notable recent polling misses, even if the margins in most reputable polls were only off by a few percentage points.

In a close race, those few points can make the difference between victory and defeat for a candidate. And this year, many of the key Senate races are within those narrow margins.

What's at stake for Trump in the US midterms?

In recent years, midterm general election polls have proven to be more accurate, in part because Trump - and his unique ability to draw disaffected Americans to the polls - was not on the ballot.

In 2022, they suggested Democrats would defy conventional expectations and avoid an electoral wipe-out despite Biden's low approval numbers.

While the party lost control of the House of Representatives, the margins were slim and Democrats maintained control of the US Senate.

While this year's primary results did provide bigger surprises, Young says that nominating contests have historically been volatile, with voters more likely to make up their minds at the last minute. It shouldn't be seen as an indication that the current crop of general election polls are off the mark.

It's common for the party of the president to have a bad night in the midterms.

One of the most notable examples in recent years came in 2010 when Republicans flipped more than 60 House seats and made gains in the Senate.

It came off the back of an economy struggling to recover from the financial crash and conservative fears over President Barack Obama's healthcare reforms. A defeat had been predicted by the polls but not quite on that scale.

The roles are reversed this time, with the Democrats in opposition and the economy on the minds of voters. But there are a number of reasons why Democrats may not have as good a night as history might suggest or as some might expect, given Trump's low approval rating.

Firstly, much of the territory in play in this election Trump conquered with ease in the presidential election just two years ago. It's like a home game for Republicans.

Then there have been changes to House election boundaries over the years that have meant fewer seats switch between parties.

Thirdly, there are fewer swing voters due to the deeper polarisation of the country. Some experts describe the electorate as calcified.

There's also the cash advantage Republicans have enjoyed which has given them hundreds of millions of dollars to spend on attack ads in the final weeks.

Obama gives a press conference the day after the heavy 2010 midterms defeat

Polling models try to capture shifting fundamentals. And a well-designed poll is built to anticipate issues that may make it less than reliable.

Individual polls do have a built-in margin of error â a statistical level of uncertainty based on the sampling size compared to the population as a whole. Taken as an aggregate, polling averages and not individual surveys can provide a more accurate prediction.

At the moment, even the kind of polling lean we saw in recent presidential elections would suggest Democrats are likely to have a good night on 3 November. And polling inaccuracies can go both ways. In last year's state and special elections, Democrats outperformed most surveys as it was their voters who turned out in unexpectedly larger numbers.

The sight of Trump holding rallies in the final days of the campaign in states he won comfortably in 2024 will have lifted Democrats' hopes that Republican internal polling has conservative candidates in trouble.

Voter voices: What issues matter most to Americans?

There's always the chance that voters change their minds right up until election day, however.

Sometimes there are late shifts that can alter the dynamics of a race. Sometimes undecided voters, which still account for a key segment of the electorate in many of these races, all break the same way.

In polling, there will always be some level of uncertainty â and managing that is the art that goes along with the science.

"I think we're doing a better job," says Young. "But part of the problem we always have is not just with the pollsters. Part of it is that we're dealing with humans. Humans are not easy."

#87. ‘I am deeply sorry’: Craig Silvey pens 9,000-word apology for child exploitation material offences

The children’s author claims he experienced trauma in childhood as he begins seven-month sentence for possessing depraved material

Warning: This article contains graphic content

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

The disgraced best-selling author Craig Silvey has claimed he experienced trauma as a child in a 9,000-word apology published after he was jailed for child exploitation offences.

The young adult novelist was sentenced on Monday to seven months’ imprisonment, after being charged in January and subsequently pleading guilty to distributing and possessing depraved child exploitation material.

The 44-year-old writer posted an article on his Substack titled “My Apology”.

He said that throughout his life he had always been able to find the right words, “but articulating the depth of my contrition and the enormity of my remorse feels beyond my aptitude for language”.

“I am guilty of these offences. I am appalled and mortified by my conduct, and I am deeply, profoundly sorry,” he wrote.

“I want to acknowledge that my engagement with this material is reprehensible and indefensible.

“It is re-traumatising to victims, and perpetuates a cycle of harm.”

Silvey, a father of three, said he betrayed the trust of thousands of people across the country, including those who had read his books, many of whom reacted to his offending with “shock, dismay, disgust, concern and disappointment”.

“I am painfully aware of the possible assumption that my offence must have been informed by a horrible and inappropriate attraction, and that my behaviour must then be connected to sinister and ulterior motivations,” he wrote.

“I assure you this is not the case at all.”

Silvey said he had carried a history of “trauma, shame and anxiety” through most of his life.

Silvey said he suffered a “crisis of mental health and a destructive, despairing breakdown” before he offended.

He was found with numerous sexually explicit photos and videos of children and adults on his laptop and mobile phone when police raided his home in Fremantle.

He also engaged in an online conversation with a person he believed shared his interest in child exploitation material, telling them he preferred boys older than five before adding that “eight was the perfect age”.

“I want to make out with little ones,” the author said in one message.

“I haven’t had any experience, I’m sad to say.

Silvey, who has been shortlisted for the Miles Franklin literary award and the International Dublin literary award, is best known for his 2009 coming‑of‑age novel Jasper Jones, which had been considered a modern Australian classic and was adapted into a feature film and several stage productions.

He has also been widely recognised for other books, including Rhubarb, Honeybee – which won the Australian Indie Book Award in 2021 – and the children’s novel Runt, which was also made into a film.

In Australia, children, young adults, parents and teachers can contact the Kids Helpline on 1800 55 1800; adult survivors can seek help at Blue Knot Foundation on 1300 657 380

#86. Indonesia's toxic haze is giving its neighbours a political headache

The haze stemming from wildfires in Indonesia has drifted into Singapore and Malaysia

The haze billowing out from wildfires in the Indonesian regions of Kalimantan, Papua and Sumatra knows no borders.

Carried by winds to nearby Singapore and Malaysia, the toxic smog has in recent weeks impacted populations across maritime South East Asia, triggering respiratory illnesses and headaches.

It's also giving the region's governments a different kind of headache - split between the mounting frustrations of their citizens and the risk of souring diplomatic ties with Indonesia.

"People are fed up, angry and anxious," Eugene Tan, associate professor of law at the Singapore Management University, tells the BBC. "The governments have to respond to them, or else what is more an external issue has become an internal one with ample headroom for politicisation."

Indonesia's wildfires are a yearly occurrence, widely associated with industrial-scale land clearing to make way for crops like oil palm.

But the blazes this year have been particularly difficult to tame due to a strong El NiÃ±o, which has exacerbated drought conditions in the country's forests and peatlands.

Whereas previous haze episodes in Singapore and Malaysia have typically lasted for a few days, this time it stretched into weeks.

In Malaysia, locals started a boycott of Indonesian products in protest against the haze, after lawmaker Syed Saddiq called for sanctions on companies linked to the wildfires and said Malaysians should "treat them as an outcast in the region".

There have also been complaints in Singapore, a country that has come to expect seamless efficiency from its leaders.

"There appears to be the view that the government dropped the ball in dealing with the haze, given that it was known that the El NiÃ±o weather phenomenon would be severe and that early warning was given," says Tan.

"Clearly, Singaporeans expect the government to decisively deal with the polluters."

Singapore's government, for its part, has made it clear that the haze problem is a priority.

Hundreds of haze shelters have been set up across the island, and masks are being distributed to children, the elderly and other vulnerable residents.

Authorities have also said they're looking to strengthen existing haze legislation and are investigating 10 Singapore-registered companies for potential links to the wildfires in Indonesia.

Last week, Singapore's foreign minister Vivian Balakrishnan met Indonesian President Prabowo Subianto in Jakarta for what the former described as "honest" and "sincere" conversations that produced "constructive approaches".

The meeting came as both countries announced they would set up a joint task force to deal with the haze - an apparent dÃ©tente after weeks of simmering bilateral tensions.

Singapore's foreign minister Vivian Balakrishnan met President Prabowo in Jakarta on Wednesday

As the haze spiralled into yet another crisis for Prabowo's beleaguered administration, some officials pushed back against criticism.

One senior official ruffled feathers when he said the wildfires were out of human control and that oxygen in neighbouring Singapore and Malaysia "also comes from our forests".

That remark was "disappointing" and "not right", according to Singaporean senior minister K Shanmugam - a rare rebuke from the city state, which prides itself on its diplomatic prowess.

"The haze is affecting our health seriously, and it's really unacceptable," Shanmugam told reporters. Since the haze impacts citizens on both sides of the border, there should not be "any bilateral issues" in addressing it, he added.

"The Indonesian public is suffering as well, and it is in Indonesia's interest as much as ours to deal with the companies that are causing these problems."

Days later, Indonesia announced it would reduce gas exports to Singapore, citing domestic energy needs - a move that many saw as retaliation to Singapore's rhetoric.

To be sure, Indonesia has faced recent electricity shortages in parts of the country. Singapore also says the reduction, which amounts to less than 2% of its total daily demand, will have little impact on its gas supply.

But the abrupt announcement came just days after Indonesia's energy minister warned that they could halt gas exports to countries that don't "respect Indonesia". That same day, Prabowo made reference to "countries that like to insult Indonesia" when talking about the wildfires.

"It's a tired playbook of using Singapore as the bogeyman, driven by the 'abang-adik' [big brother-little brother] syndrome," Tan says, referring to a historical dynamic that Singapore's leaders have long tried to shrug off: the expectation that it should defer to its larger neighbours.

"Singapore leaders expressing concerns about the haze and how it affects the people of both countries have been taken to mean that Singapore has been disrespectful to its neighbour."

Schools were closed in parts of Malaysia when haze reached unhealthy levels this month

In contrast, Malaysia's official rhetoric on the haze has been relatively muted.

Its foreign minister Mohamad Hasan has ruled out lodging a formal diplomatic protest with Indonesia or taking legal action, saying such actions "would mean we were prepared to sever relations".

For now, Malaysia has agreed to explore a memorandum of understanding with Indonesia to strengthen wildfire prevention efforts.

It would also rely on diplomatic mechanisms, Mohamad said - like the Asean Agreement on Transboundary Haze Pollution.

The agreement, which came into effect in 2003, commits members of the Association of Southeast Asian Nations (Asean) to collaborate on tackling the recurring haze.

But many see it as toothless, as it is unable to punish agricultural companies linked to wildfires or to force individual governments to do so.

The agreement has turned out to be "just not worth the paper it was signed on", says James Chin, professor of Asian studies at the University of Tasmania.

"The moment the haze disappears, the moment the political pressure's off, it's back to business as usual."

In 2016, Asean countries had set a goal to eliminate haze in the region by 2020. When that deadline drifted quietly by, they set a new target of achieving the goal by 2030 instead.

Complicating Malaysia's position is the fact that it is also a major palm oil producer. The commodity is often linked to commercial "slash-and-burn" practices that are widely blamed for the region's wildfires.

Malaysia has at times dealt with acute haze stemming from blazes on local land.

In 2019, when Malaysia pointed to hotspots in Indonesia's peatlands as the cause of the haze shrouding the region, Indonesian authorities pointed the finger back at Malaysia, claiming the smog came from their side.

"As long as we place a lot of importance on oil palm planting, [the haze] is going to recur," says Ei Sun Oh, principal adviser at the Pacific Research Centre of Malaysia.

And even if Malaysia were to speak up, Oh adds, it's unlikely to wield the same "leverage" over Indonesia as Singapore, which is Indonesia's largest foreign investor and an offshore financial hub for many wealthy Indonesians.

For now, Malaysia "doesn't want to unduly provoke Indonesia", says Oh.

"The government is just wringing their hands till the wind blows [the problem] over. Because eventually it will blow over."

#83. Beijing Queer film festival cancelled after harassment from authorities

Forums for discussion of feminist and LGBTQ+ views face growing intolerance from Chinese state, say experts

Beijing’s annual independent queer film festival has been cancelled after what is understood to have been a campaign of sustained harassment from authorities.

Beijing Queer film festival, which was due to begin on Friday in China’s capital, is one of the country’s oldest independent film festivals.

In a statement posted on Instagram late on Thursday, the organisers said: “We regret to inform you that the Love Queer Film Week … has been cancelled. We sincerely apologise for any inconvenience and disappointment that the cancellation of this event may cause to those who planned to participate or had been looking forward to the film week.”

Police had increased pressure on the festival through repeated phone calls in recent weeks, it is understood.

Founded in 2001 by a group of Peking University students, the festival grew to become the only film festival in mainland China to offer a public forum for films and discussions exploring LGBTQ+ issues.

The Chinese authorities have grown increasingly intolerant of open discussion of feminist or LGBTQ+ issues. Film festivals in particular attract particular scrutiny because they provide an opportunity to platform narratives that sit outside the Communist party’s worldview.

Beijing Independent film festival, China’s most prominent independent film festival, was closed down in 2014 after the authorities detained the founders and shut off the electricity supply to the venue. Last year, an independent Chinese film festival that was due to be held in New York was cancelled after harassment from the Chinese authorities, an incident which raised concern about transnational repression.

Maya Wang, the deputy Asia director for Human Rights Watch, said: “The Chinese government under Xi has been relentlessly tightening social and ideological control, and so any activities outside of those sanctioned by the party-state are subjected to intensifying scrutiny and often also official harassment.

“The authorities have put more pressure on what little is left of China’s civil society, as well as the LGBT communities and independent film-makers. A queer film festival basically faces all of these overlapping pressures.”

Hongwei Bao, a professor of Chinese studies at the University of St Andrews who focuses on LGBT issues, said that the festival was “a beacon for LGBT resilience” in China.

“The shutdown of the Love Queer Cinema Week has a chilling affect on the Chinese queer community,” Bao said.

In June this year, screenings of films with LGBTQ+ themes that were scheduled to be held in Beijing during Pride month at the Institut Français, a French cultural centre, were cancelled because of police harassment.

Despite the increased pressure, Beijing Queer film festival remains a popular event. Several international film-makers whose work had been selected for the festival had travelled to China to participate. Last year, the festival received 200 volunteer applications, four times the number received in 2024.

Beijing’s public security bureau could not be reached for comment.

#82. Why a US-Russia showdown left Morocco celebrating

Morocco claims Western Sahara as part of its territory

Morocco has welcomed the end of a decades-long UN peacekeeping mission in Western Sahara, after a diplomatic showdown between the US and Russia left the Security Council unable to agree on its future.

The council failed on Friday to renew the mandate of the UN Mission for the Referendum in Western Sahara (Minurso) after Russia vetoed a US-backed proposal. A rival Russian draft to extend the mission also failed to secure enough votes.

The decision raises questions about the future of Africa's longest territorial dispute and the fragile ceasefire between Morocco, which claims Western Sahara, and the Polisario Front which has been fighting for the territory's independence for more than 50 years.

Western Sahara, on Africa's north-west coast, was a Spanish colony until 1975. It has significant phosphate reserves and valuable fishing grounds along its Atlantic coast.

As Spain prepared to withdraw, both Morocco and Mauritania claimed the territory.

The International Court of Justice found historical ties of allegiance but no territorial sovereignty that overrode the Sahrawi people's right to self-determination.

Morocco's then King Hassan II responded by organising the Green March. On 6 November 1975 about 350,000 Moroccan civilians entered the territory in a mass demonstration intended to put pressure on Spain.

Days later, Spain signed the Madrid Accords with Morocco and Mauritania, agreeing to transfer administrative control. Morocco took the northern two-thirds and Mauritania the southern third. The agreement did not settle the question of sovereignty.

The Polisario Front, an independence movement founded in 1973, opposed the deal. It saw it as replacing Spanish colonial rule with Moroccan and Mauritanian control.

Mauritania renounced its claim in 1979, but Morocco continued fighting Polisario.

Algeria became Polisario main regional ally, providing military support and hosting its leadership and thousands of Sahrawi refugees in camps near the city of Tindouf.

The North African country says its position is based on the right to self-determination, while Morocco sees Western Sahara as an integral part of its territory.

Morocco and Algeria fought a brief border war in 1963 over disagreements about territory inherited from French colonial rule. Their relationship has remained tense, with Western Sahara at the centre of their dispute.

After 16 years of fighting, Morocco and Polisario agreed to a UN-brokered ceasefire in 1991, with the territory divided by a sand wall, or berm, to keep the rival forces apart.

The UN established Minurso to monitor the ceasefire and organise a referendum on the territory's future. Sahrawis were meant to choose between independence and integration with Morocco.

The vote was scheduled for 1992 but never took place. The two sides could not agree on who would be eligible to participate, with disputes over voter identification becoming a major obstacle.

In 2007, Morocco put forward a proposal for autonomy, which would see Sahrawis managing their internal affairs, while Rabat retained control over defence and foreign relations.

Thousands of Moroccans marched into Western Sahara in 1975 in what became known as the Green March

The Polisario Front and Algeria rejected the plan, insisting on a referendum that included independence as an option.

Years of UN-sponsored negotiations failed to produce a breakthrough.

The ceasefire broke down in November 2020, when Polisario said it was resuming armed struggle.

But following a long diplomatic campaign by Morocco, the UN Security Council voted in 2025 in favour of a resolution backing the autonomy plan as the "most feasible" solution for the disputed territory.

While most Sahrawis live in the part of Western Sahara controlled by Morocco, tens of thousands are estimated to be in refugee camps in Algeria.

The US proposed extending Minurso's mandate for another year, but its draft went beyond the usual renewal.

It would have removed the word "referendum" from the mission's name and made Morocco's autonomy proposal the sole basis for further negotiations.

The draft received the support of 11 of the Security Council's 15 members, including France and the UK but Russia vetoed it, arguing that it was too favourable to Morocco.

Russia then proposed extending Minurso's mandate without changing its terms. That draft also failed, receiving only two votes in favour.

Minurso's mandate expired at the end of Saturday, 31 October. The UN said the following day that the mission had entered a liquidation period, with a reduced team overseeing the transfer of military equipment and other assets.

The UN mission has been in Western Sahara since 1991

UN Secretary-General AntÃ³nio Guterres said the Sahrawi people's right to self-determination must be respected and warned of the risk of renewed hostilities.

The Polisario Front condemned the US proposal. Its representative, Sidi Omar, thanked Russia for vetoing it, saying it would "torpedo the foundations of the UN peace process" and threaten regional and international peace.

Morocco, meanwhile, said the mission's personnel and equipment should leave the territory in the coming days, arguing that Western Sahara issue should no longer be considered by the Security Council.

Minurso had 155 military personnel and was expected to cost $62m (Â£46m) in the financial year beginning in June, according to a UN report.

Morocco's royal palace welcomed the failure to renew Minurso's mandate.

In Laayoune, the territory's largest city, footage showed people celebrating, waving Moroccan flags and displaying portraits of King Mohammed VI.

"The decision is an opportunity for Rabat to challenge what it sees as an international mechanism that has failed to resolve the dispute," Issam Laaroussi, a Moroccan expert in international relations, tells the BBC.

He said Morocco would now have more room to push its autonomy proposal and shift negotiations away from a referendum on independence towards autonomy under Moroccan sovereignty.

The decision does not, however, change Western Sahara's legal status or amount to international recognition of Moroccan sovereignty.

Western Sahara remains on the UN list of non-self-governing territories, while the African Union officially recognises its independence, as the Sahrawi Arab Democratic Republic (SADR).

But the decision comes after several countries backed the position of Morocco, which in recent years has made securing international support for its claim a central part of its foreign policy.

In 2020, the US recognised Moroccan sovereignty over Western Sahara as part of an agreement to establish diplomatic relations between Morocco and Israel.

France also backed Morocco's autonomy plan as the basis for resolving the dispute in 2024.

Thousands of Sahrawis live in refugee camps in Algeria

Morocco rejoined the African Union in 2017, more than three decades after leaving its predecessor, the Organisation of African Unity, in protest at the SADR's admission.

Since returning, Rabat has sought to build support for its position from within the organisation. At least 10 African countries have opened diplomatic missions in the territory in support of Rabat.

"Morocco is not necessarily seeking to abandon the UN process, but to change the framework for negotiations and draw greater support from regional organisations," says Laaroussi.

Addressing a special joint session of parliament on Monday, House of Representatives Speaker Ahmed Touizi hailed the end of Minurso as a victory for the country's diplomacy.

He said the country should now move from "managing the dispute to implementing its autonomy plan under Moroccan sovereignty, while pressing ahead with development projects in the territory."

The fear is that Minurso's departure could lead to renewed fighting.

The mission has monitored military developments and helped reduce the risk of escalation.

Aid organisation Oxfam warned that losing its monitoring and de-escalation role could undermine stability, with serious consequences for civilians.

Laaroussi admits that the mission's departure could create security gaps.

"Minurso's departure does not create the original conflict, because the conflict is already there, but it could aggravate an already fragile security environment," he says.

The UN decision has also fuelled nationalist rhetoric online.

Several Moroccan users have shared AI-generated images of themselves in military uniform, declaring their readiness to fight for Morocco's sovereignty over Western Sahara.

In Morocco, Western Sahara is widely regarded as a national cause. Political parties that are often divided on other issues are united in supporting the country's claim to sovereignty over the territory.

Foreign Minister Nasser Bourita has said the end of the mission does not mean the end of the ceasefire, and that Rabat remains committed to it "within respect of its security and sovereignty."

Polisario has not said what it will do next.

Any resumption of fighting could well draw in Algeria, as tensions with Morocco remain high.

According to the Stockholm International Peace Research Institute (SIPRI), both countries significantly increased military spending last year, with SIPRI citing their dispute over Western Sahara as a key driver.

Algeria spent an estimated $25.4bn, more than four time Morocco's $6.3bn.

Morocco wants tourists to visit Western Sahara. Some say it's tightening its control

Proposition 40 would impose a one-time 5% tax on the wealth of Californians whose net worth exceeds $1 billion, with most of the revenue dedicated to replacing federal funding cuts to healthcare.

#80. Up in the Clouds With the Pope, It’s Still Commercial

#79. If hackers can take control of a supermarket freezer, how vulnerable is other tech?

Supermarkets across Europe have been hit by a suspected cyber-attack affecting their fridges and freezers, and raising concern about the vulnerabilities of internet-connected hardware from industrial machinery to humble domestic appliances.

Smart devices which are connected to the internet can include fridges, central heating, and even washing machines.

And while that makes them easier to control remotely, in theory, any device which goes online can potentially be exploited by malicious actors.

However, that doesn't mean hackers can easily take control of it, and big brands generally go to great lengths to make sure their tech is secure.

Exploits have been found before in some devices. In 2015, researchers demonstrated how a vulnerability in a Samsung smart fridge could allow them to intercept internet traffic.

But there's no evidence domestic appliances are being targeted in the cyber-attacks which targeted supermarkets in November.

Ciaran Martin, former chief executive of the UK's National Cyber Security Centre (NCSC) says manufacturers needed to do more to make connected devices secure.

"There's a lot more that we need to demand of big tech to give us safer devices and products in the first place," he said.

He advised consumers to keep devices updated and enable multi-factor authentication wherever possible.

"I know it's a pain, but it only takes a few seconds," he said.

Since April 2024, UK law has required manufacturers of certain consumer smart devices to meet minimum cyber security standards, including requirements around passwords and security updates.

The NCSC said it was working with international partners and industry to understand the supermarket incidents, but it was unaware of any impact on the UK.

However, researchers from UK Cyber Defence have identified more than 800 refrigeration and building-control interfaces associated with British retailers that can be accessed over the public internet.

That doesn't necessarily mean they can be hacked, but it could leave them exposed to attackers exploiting weak passwords or software vulnerabilities.

The government has already held talks with senior food retailers about cyber resilience, following earlier attacks on supermarkets.

Helen Dickinson, chief executive of the British Retail Consortium, has said cyber incidents are a major concern for retailers, with businesses working alongside the NCSC to share intelligence and strengthen their defences.

It would not be the first time that hacks have raised concern about impact on food supply.

When UK supermarkets M&S and the Co-op were targeted by ransomware hackers in May 2025, the retailers struggled to stock some shop shelves with fresh deliveries.

Customers across the UK complained of empty shelves in the days following the attack.

Tom Hegel, a researcher at cyber-security firm SentinelOne, said retailers must be clearer about responsibility.

"The question retailers need to answer is who owns the security of that equipment, not just who repairs it," he said.

He said internet connectivity can make everyday technology more convenient, but security needed to keep pace.

"Once an attacker can operate those controls, legitimate functions such as defrosting become tools for disruption," he said.

"Changing a setting can destroy physical stock, not just interrupt a computer system."

Outside of the home, connected tech is used to remotely manage industrial machinery and vehicles.

Cyber security expert Ken Munro pointed to a 2015 demonstration in which researchers remotely hacked a Jeep through its internet-connected entertainment system, prompting improvements in vehicle security.

"Fortunately, the vehicle industry woke up to this and fixed the problem," he said.

Mobile connections are also widely used to monitor equipment in power plants and water networks, allowing engineers to manage systems remotely.

Munro said UK utilities were generally good at securing these connections, but standards varied around the world.

His biggest concern was smaller, poorly funded utility companies which may still be relying on older, vulnerable equipment.

Without proper protections, "any factory or equipment" connected to the internet - ranging from carmakers to electricity or transport infrastructure - could face similar risks, said Olivier Daloy, a cybersecurity expert at Zscaler.

#74. How crypto hacking funds isolated North Korea’s nuclear programme

Cryptocurrency theft is estimated to generate half of the country’s foreign currency income

North Korea plays by its own rules. The isolated, secretive and autocratic country is largely cut off from the world’s economic system – but when it comes to generating foreign earnings it relies on some of the world’s most sophisticated hacking.

Experts, such as TRM Labs, quickly attributed the extraordinary $6.3bn (£4.7bn) Binance cryptocurrency theft to TraderTraitor, an elite North Korean hacking unit that has also been known as the Lazarus Group. Though the link may not be proven yet, the pattern of behaviour is familiar.

North Korea has a history of cryptocurrency theft dating back to 2017, focusing on the sector’s relatively lax security controls as a way of stealing money and generating revenues for its military, including its nuclear weapons programme.

Kim Jong-un, its dictatorial ruler, controls a stockpile of about 50 nuclear warheads, and has been supplying troops and weapons to help Russia in its war in Ukraine. But North Korea is also extremely poor: 40% of the population are undernourished according to the UN and nearly one in five of its children are stunted.

A UN panel of experts, reporting to the security council, said in the spring of 2024 that it was reviewing “58 suspected cyber-attacks” by North Korea “on cryptocurrency-related companies between 2017 and 2023” worth $3bn.

The panel said the cash was estimated to generate half of North Korea’s foreign currency income. One unnamed country went further with its estimate: the stolen billions fund “40% of the weapons of mass destruction programmes”.

The suspected crypto hacks amounted to as many as 17 in 2023 alone: thefts valued at $750m, and disturbingly, the sums involved are only increasing. The world’s largest previous crypto hack took place in February 2025, when $1.5bn was stolen from the Bybit exchange – the work, the FBI said, of TraderTraitor.

Ciaran Martin, a former head of the UK’s National Cyber Security Centre, argues that North Korea appears to have targeted crypto because traditional banks have developed sophisticated defences that the fast-growing and lightly regulated sector has yet to match.

“Over the last decade, hackers have walked off with nine- or 10-figure sums from crypto hacks on more than 30 occasions. The number of traditional banks who have suffered a loss of more than $100m as a result of a cyber-attack in the same period is zero or one,” Martin said.

North Korea was behind one of the last large cyber thefts on a traditional bank – an attack on Bangladesh Bank in February 2016, where North Korean hackers tried to illicitly move $951m from an account the bank had with the Federal Reserve Bank of New York, taking advantage of time differences between the two.

A piece of luck prevented most of the money from being sent to an intermediate account in the Philippines. The corresponding bank was based on Jupiter Street, which shares its first word with the name of a sanctioned Iranian ship, so most of the transfers were held up. In the end, $81m was stolen.

The isolated state has long been accused of engaging in criminal activities to survive, including the forgery of high-quality $100 bills, known as “supernotes” in the 1990s, and the production and trafficking of crystal meth for export in the 2000s.

The UK has been rarely targeted. In 2019, North Korean hackers did try to take control of cash machines linked to 56 financial institutions to make them pay out sums well in excess of normal. British officials spotted what was afoot, alerted the banks and the hack was quietly suppressed with no media coverage.

The irony is that the internet in North Korea is severely restricted, with access denied to the general population. Maths and science are pushed at school, however, and at university some of the best students are recruited into hacker groups, such as Lazarus, operating under the auspices of the country’s Reconnaissance General Bureau spy agency.

Hacking is closely tied to the all-powerful state. In December 2014, it emerged, to the embarrassment of Sony Pictures, that film stars Jennifer Lawrence and Amy Adams were paid less than their male counterparts in American Hustle. It was one of thousands of embarrassing emails leaked from the Hollywood studio through a hack.

The attack, by North Korea’s Lazarus Group, was conducted because Sony had funded a film, The Interview: a Seth Rogen comedy that depicted the assassination of Kim Jong-un. Amid the chaos of the revelations, Sony tried to cancel the movie, then had to U-turn on its retreat, releasing it online and to a handful of cinemas, after being criticised for caving in to North Korean pressure.

#69. Binance resumes customer withdrawals after record $6.3bn hack

Cryptocurrency exchange says 90% of losses covered as FBI blames North Korean state-sponsored group TraderTraitor

The cryptocurrency exchange Binance has started to allow its customers to withdraw their funds from the platform, as it recovers from a record $6.3bn (£4.7bn) hack.

The exchange was hit by the theft on Thursday, which has since been described as the biggest heist in history, with experts identifying North Korea as the likely culprit.

Binance initially froze withdrawals as it investigated the breach, but on Sunday announced that it had began a phased reopening after 2pm Singapore time.

In the first phase people holding the ethereum, USDT and USDC tokens on the ethereum blockchain were able to withdraw their funds, as well as some others held on the BNB Smart Chain blockchain. Later on Sunday, dealing in the Dogecoin and Avalanche currencies resumed on their own platforms.

The platform said it had replenished 90% of the ⁠amount stolen. The remaining 10% of tokens were less liquid, meaning they ⁠may take ​longer to buy, the company said in a post on X on Saturday.

Richard Teng, Binance’s co-chief executive, said separately that the company was “committed to covering the ⁠remaining 10%” and users would not bear the financial impact of the hack.

A weekly dive in to how technology is shaping our lives

It came as the FBI on Saturday confirmed earlier suspicions from private investigators and analysts that a North Korea-affiliated group called TraderTraitor was behind the hack.

TraderTraitor is believed to be part of the Lazarus Group, which gained notoriety in 2014 when it was accused of hacking into Sony Pictures as revenge for The Interview, a film that mocked the North Korean leader Kim Jong-un.

“The Federal Bureau of Investigation is releasing information advising the Democratic People’s Republic of Korea (North Korea) was responsible for the theft of more than $6bn in virtual assets from cryptocurrency exchange Binance, on or about October 29,” the law enforcement agency said in a statement.

The FBI added that it had observed “TraderTraitor actors” converting stolen assets into ethereum, and expected those assets to be laundered further by converting them using decentralised finance and instant swap services.

It said it was urging private sector entities “to block transactions with or derived from addresses TraderTraitor actors” were “using to launder these stolen assets”.

Teng said on Friday that the breach was a “complex attack” involving a compromised laptop and malicious software designed to delete evidence of its activity, making it ‌harder to trace and investigate.

Binance said it had set aside about $6.34bn of company funds to cover the losses. “Our commitment remains the same: Binance will fully cover the financial impact of the funds stolen in this incident,” the company said.

The money spent rebuying tokens came from using part of its $1bn secure asset fund for users, a bitcoin reserve it holds to protect customers in the event of security breaches, as well as some ⁠of its futures insurance fund and own corporate treasury, the company said ​on X.

The attack on Binance came as many observers express worries that the ever-expanding capabilities of artificial intelligence models could compromise cryptography and IT security protocols.

Bitcoin plunged nearly 5% after the news of the hack, while ethereum dropped nearly 6% and BNB, native to Binance, tumbled nearly 10%.

The sheer scale of the hack has put a spotlight on the capacity of North Korea – which faces extensive international sanctions over its nuclear and missile programmes – to find illicit means to capture revenue.
